Time-harmonic wave propagation in junctions of two periodic half-spaces
Pierre Amenoagbadji, Sonia Fliss, Patrick Joly

Abstract
We are interested in the Helmholtz equation in a junction of two periodic half-spaces. When the overall medium is periodic in the direction of the interface, Fliss and Joly (2019) proposed a method which consists in applying a partial Floquet-Bloch transform along the interface, to obtain a family of waveguide problems parameterized by the Floquet variable. In this paper, we consider two model configurations where the medium is no longer periodic in the direction of the interface. Inspired by the works of Gérard-Varet and Masmoudi (2011, 2012), and Blanc, Le Bris, and Lions (2015), we use the fact that the overall medium has a so-called quasiperiodic structure, in the sense that it is the restriction of a higher dimensional periodic medium. Accordingly, the Helmholtz equation is lifted onto a higher dimensional problem with coefficients that are periodic along the interface. This periodicity property allows us to adapt the tools previously developed for periodic media. However, the augmented PDE is elliptically degenerate (in the sense of the principal part of its differential operator) and thus more delicate to analyse.



1
Introduction and description of the method

1.1
Presentation of the model problem

Initially motivated by solid state theory in the 1940s
[
27
]
, the study of periodic media has since then sparked some significant interest in the mathematical physics literature, especially with the recent advent of photonic crystals
[
21
,
22
,
29
,
35
]
. When it comes to wave propagation, one of the most remarkable properties of such structures from a practical point of view is the possibility of having
band gaps
, that is, frequency ranges for which waves cannot propagate (we refer to
[
30
,
29
]
for a mathematical explanation). This feature is of particular interest in micro or nano-technology, for building optical filters or wave localization devices. In order to understand the underlying phenomena and to design these applications, it is important to have efficient numerical tools to solve the time-harmonic wave equation in presence of periodic media.

Figure 1
:
Juxtaposition of arbitrary periodic half-spaces

This paper is devoted to time-harmonic wave propagation in presence of two periodic half-spaces
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\pm}:=\{{\bm{x}}=({x},{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ /\ \pm{x}>0\}
(see Figure
1
). More precisely, let
\sigma:=\{{\bm{x}}=({x},{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ /\ x=0\}
denote the interface between
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-}
. The canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is
\{{\bm{e}}_{x},{\bm{e}}_{z}\}
. We are interested in
finding
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l@{\quad}l}\displaystyle-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\nabla}\,\mathbb{A}\,\nabla u-\rho\,\omega^{2}\,u&0&\textnormal{in}\ \ \mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\cup\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-},\\[8.0pt]
\displaystyle\llbracket\mathbb{A}\,\nabla u\cdot{\bm{e}}_{x}\rrbracket_{\sigma}&g&\textnormal{on}\ \ \sigma,\end{array}\right.

(
\mathscr{P}
)

where the presence of some (arbitrarily small) absorption is assumed (see Remark
1.1.b
), namely

\text{the frequency }\omega\text{ satisfies }\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits\omega>0.

(1.1)

The overall medium is represented by the tensor
\mathbb{A}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})
and the coefficient
\rho\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. We assume that
\mathbb{A}
is symmetric, and that

\displaystyle\exists\;a,r>0,\quad\forall\;{\bm{x}},\bm{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\qquad 0<a\;|\bm{\xi}|^{2}\leq\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\bm{\xi}}\;\mathbb{A}({\bm{x}})\;\bm{\xi}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad 0<r\leq\rho({\bm{x}}).

(1.2)

Let
{\bm{p}}^{\pm}_{1},{\bm{p}}^{\pm}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
be linearly independent vectors. The tensor
\mathbb{A}
(
resp.
the coefficient
\rho
) is assumed to coincide in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\pm}
with a continuous function
\mathbb{A}^{\pm}
(
resp.

\rho^{\pm}
) which is
\mathbb{Z}{\bm{p}}^{\pm}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}{\bm{p}}^{\pm}_{2}
–periodic, as illustrated in Figure
1
. The exact structural properties of
(\mathbb{A},\rho)
considered in this paper, namely the assumptions on
(\mathbb{A}^{\pm},\rho^{\pm})
and
({\bm{p}}^{\pm}_{1},{\bm{p}}^{\pm}_{2})
, are detailed in Section
2.1
and shown in Figures
3
and
4
.

In (

P

), we use the notation
\llbracket\bm{w}\cdot{\bm{e}}_{x}\rrbracket_{\sigma}\in H^{-1/2}(\sigma)
for the jump accross
\sigma
of the normal component of a function
\bm{w}\in[L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})]^{2}
such that
\bm{w}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\pm}}\in H(\mathop{{\hbox{\rm div}}}\nolimits;\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\pm})
:

\llbracket\bm{w}\cdot{\bm{e}}_{x}\rrbracket_{\sigma}:=(\bm{w}^{-}\cdot{\bm{e}}_{x})|_{\sigma}-(\bm{w}^{+}\cdot{\bm{e}}_{x})|_{\sigma},\quad\textnormal{with}\quad\bm{w}^{\pm}:=\bm{w}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\pm}}.

(1.3)

Finally
g\in H^{-1/2}(\sigma)
is a given jump data (see Remark
1.1.a
).

Remark 1.1
.

(\textit{a})
.

We have considered a jump condition in (

P

) for simplicity. This condition allows to reduce (

P

) to an interface equation, as shown in Section
4.3
(see also Figure
13
). In Section
7.1
, we explain how the method in this paper extends to the case of a volume source term (that is, a right-hand side in the volume equation of (

P

)).

(\textit{b})
.

For real-valued frequencies
\omega
, one expects that (

P

) does not admit any solution in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, due to a lack of sufficient decay in
|{\bm{x}}|
at infinity, caused by wave propagation up to infinity. Moreover, uniqueness of a solution in
H^{1}_{\textit{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
does not hold in general. One classical way to define the physical solution for wave propagation problems in unbounded media is to add a so-called
radiation condition
, which describes the behavior of the solution at infinity. In the homogeneous case, that is, when
\mathbb{A}
and
\rho
are constant, this is the so-called Sommerfeld radiation condition
[
36
]
. However, to our knowledge, such a radiation condition is not known in presence of unbounded periodic media.

The physical solution can also be defined using the
limiting absorption principle
, which consists in (
1
) adding an imaginary part to
\omega
(called absorption), and in (
2
) studying the limit process as
\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits\omega\to 0
. If the limit in a certain sense exists, then it is the physical solution. The limiting absorption principle is well-established for time-harmonic wave propagation problems in unbounded media that are homogeneous or stratified outside a bounded domain; see for instance
[
1
,
10
,
40
,
38
]
. More recently, it has been successfully applied to periodic closed waveguides
[
11
,
20
,
15
,
16
]
, periodic layers
[
24
,
25
,
26
]
(see also
[
5
]
), and to
n
–dimensional fully periodic media with
n\geq 2

[
33
,
34
,
32
]
. However, as far as we know, there is no complete answer for transmission problems between periodic half-spaces. This is the reason why we assume the presence of some arbitrarily small but non-vanishing absorption (namely (
1.1
)), which is an essential step in understanding the non-absorbing case. We also refer to Section
7.3
for further discussion on the limiting absorption principle.

Under Assumptions (
1.1
) and (
1.2
), it follows from Lax-Milgram’s theorem that Problem (

P

) admits a unique solution
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Moreover, if
g\in L^{2}(\sigma)
is compactly supported, then a Combes-Thomas estimate
[
8
]
(see also Remark
4.11
) allows to prove that this solution decays exponentially at infinity in all directions, namely

\displaystyle\exists\;c,\,\alpha>0,\quad\big\|u\,\exp(\alpha\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits\omega\,|{\bm{x}}|)\big\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq c\;\|g\|_{L^{2}(\sigma)}.

(1.4)

To solve (

P

), a naive method relying on this decay estimate would consist in truncating the computational domain at a certain distance related to
\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits\omega
, with homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions for instance. However the accuracy of such a method is prone to deterioration as
\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits\omega\to 0
. Worse, if
g
is not compactly supported, then only the exponential decay in the direction normal to the interface is guaranteed:

\exists\;c,\,\alpha>0,\quad\big\|u\,\exp(\alpha\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits\omega\,|{x}|)\big\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq c\;\|g\|_{L^{2}(\sigma)}.

(1.5)

Using the properties of
\mathbb{A}
and
\rho
, our goal is to develop a rigorous numerical method which allows to deal with the unboundedness of the domain, and which we hope will remain robust as
\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits\omega
tends to
0
. In the case where
\mathbb{A}
and
\rho
are periodic in the direction of the interface
\sigma
, such a method has been proposed by
[
12
]
, in the spirit of
[
3
,
13
,
14
]
. The underlying idea is to apply a Floquet-Bloch transform with respect to the variable
{z}
along the interface, to obtain a family of closed waveguide problems parameterized by the Floquet variable, and which can be solved using the Dirichlet-to-Neumann (DtN) approach proposed in
[
11
,
23
]
.

Our goal is to extend the method in
[
12
]
to the case where the global medium represented by
\mathbb{A}
and
\rho
is no longer periodic in the direction of the interface. We shall use the crucial (but non-obvious) observation that the medium has a
quasiperiodic
structure along the interface, namely, that it is the restriction of a higher dimensional periodic structure. Accordingly, the idea is to interpret (

P

) as the
“restriction”
of an
augmented
boundary value problem in higher dimensions, where periodicity along the interface is recovered. This so-called
lifting approach
allows one to adapt the ideas in
[
12
]
, but comes with the price that the augmented partial differential equation (PDE) is elliptically degenerate (in the sense of the principal part of its differential operator), and hence more complicated to analyse and approximate.

Although the lifting approach already appears in the works by Kozlov
[
28
]
, the idea of using this approach for periodic half-spaces came to us mainly from the more recent papers
[
17
,
18
,
4
]
. In these papers, the lifting approach has been used in the homogenization setting (that is for instance, when the period of the structure is negligible with respect to the wavelength) in presence of a boundary or an interface. The lifting approach has also been used in
[
6
,
39
]
for the homogenization of quasicrystals. However, to our knowledge, it seems that this idea has never been used for the analysis or simulation of wave propagation phenomena. The first approach in this direction has been developed in
[
2
]
for wave propagation in
1
–dimensional quasiperiodic structures.

1.2
Formal description of the method

We provide a brief and formal description of the method developed in this paper. In what follows, the generic
3
–dimensional space variable is denoted by
\textnormal{{x}}=({x},{z}_{1},{z}_{2})
. The canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is denoted by
\{\textnormal{{e}}_{x},\textnormal{{e}}_{1},\textnormal{{e}}_{2}\}
.

Depending on the restrictions
(\mathbb{A}^{\pm},\rho^{\pm})
and the periodicity vectors
({\bm{p}}^{\pm}_{1},{\bm{p}}^{\pm}_{2})
,
\mathbb{A}
and
\rho
may not be periodic with respect to
{z}
. Nevertheless, under some suitable assumptions on
(\mathbb{A}^{\pm},\rho^{\pm})
and
({\bm{p}}^{\pm}_{1},{\bm{p}}^{\pm}_{2})
that we shall introduce in Section
2.1
(see also Figures
3
and
4
), we have from Section
2.2
that

\operatorname{a.e.}\;{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\mathbb{A}({\bm{x}})=\mathbb{A}_{p}({\mathbb{\Theta}}\,{\bm{x}})\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\rho({\bm{x}})=\rho_{p}({\mathbb{\Theta}}\,{\bm{x}}),

(1.6)

for some
{\mathbb{\Theta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 2}
. The tensor
\mathbb{A}_{p}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})
(
resp.
the coefficient
\rho_{p}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
) coincides in the half-space
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm}:=\{\textnormal{{x}}=({x},{z}_{1},{z}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\ /\ \pm{x}>0\}
with a
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
–periodic function
\mathbb{A}^{\pm}_{p}
(
resp.

\rho^{\pm}_{p}
), as Figures
5
and
6
show. As a consequence, contrary to
\mathbb{A}
and
\rho
,
\mathbb{A}_{p}
and
\rho_{p}
are
1
–periodic with respect to the variables
({z}_{1},{z}_{2})
of the interface
\Sigma
between
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
and
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{-}
:

\Sigma:=\{\textnormal{{x}}=({x},{z}_{1},{z}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\ /\ {x}=0\}.

(1.7)

Equation (
1.6
) shows the
quasiperiodic
nature of
\mathbb{A}
and
\rho
, and suggests seeking the solution
u
of (

P

) as the restriction of a
3
D function
U
along the hyperplane
{\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\mathbb{R}^{2}
, that is:

\displaystyle\forall\;{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad u({\bm{x}})=U({\mathbb{\Theta}}\,{\bm{x}}).

(1.8)

The extension
U
shall be characterized as the solution of a 3D
“augmented”
problem with periodic coefficients
\mathbb{A}_{p}
and
\rho_{p}
. In order to construct such a problem for
U
, we formally use a chain rule, which links the partial derivatives of
u
with those of
U
: if
\bm{\nabla}:=\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut(\partial_{x}\ \ \partial_{{z}_{1}}\ \ \partial_{{z}_{2}})}
denotes the
3
D gradient operator, then given
F\in\mathscr{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, one has

\displaystyle\forall\;{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\big[\nabla F({\mathbb{\Theta}}\cdot)\big]({\bm{x}})=\big[\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\bm{\nabla}F\big]({\mathbb{\Theta}}\,{\bm{x}}).

(1.9)

Using the ansatz (
1.8
) and the chain rule (
1.9
) in the volume equation satisfied by
u
suggests to introduce

\displaystyle-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\bm{\nabla}}{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\bm{\nabla}\,U-\rho_{p}\,\omega^{2}\,U=0\quad\textnormal{in}\ \ \mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\cup\mathbb{R}^{3}_{-}.

(1.10a)

In addition, the jump condition in (

P

) on the line
\sigma
may be formally lifted into a jump condition on the plane
\Sigma
, which is the interface defined by (
1.7
). This can be written as

\displaystyle\llbracket({\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\bm{\nabla}\,U)\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{x}\rrbracket_{\Sigma}=G.

(1.10b)

Here,
\llbracket{\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\bm{W}\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{x}\rrbracket_{\Sigma}:=({\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\bm{W}^{-}\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{x})|_{\Sigma}-({\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\bm{W}^{+}\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{x})|_{\Sigma}
, with
\bm{W}^{\pm}:=\bm{W}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm}}
for any
\bm{W}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and the data
G:\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
must formally satisfy the following condition

\operatorname{a.e.}\;{\bm{x}}=(0,{z})\in\sigma,\quad G({\mathbb{\Theta}}\,{\bm{x}})=g({\bm{x}}),

(1.11)

by consistency with the jump condition in (

P

). The appropriate functional framework to study the augmented problem (
1.10
) is developed in Section
3
.

\mathbb{R}^{3}
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,1)
Step (
a
)
\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)^{2}
Step (
b
)
(-1,1)\times(0,1)^{2}
Step (
c
)
\textnormal{{e}}_{x}
\textnormal{{e}}_{1}
\textnormal{{e}}_{2}

Figure 2
:
Reduction of the domains through the steps (
a
), (
b
), and (
c
).

The advantage of Problem (
1.10
) lies in the periodicity properties of
(\mathbb{A}_{p},\rho_{p})
along
\Sigma
, which are exploited in Section
4
to reduce computations to a bounded domain. More precisely, the procedure is divided into three steps, each of which is devoted to bounding the domain in one direction, as illustrated in Figure
2
:

(\textit{a})

Periodic augmented jump data
. The condition (
1.11
) offers some great latitude in choosing
G
. If
g
is smooth enough, then one obvious pick would be a
G
that is constant in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–direction for instance, namely
G(0,{z}_{1},{z}_{2}):=g(0,{z}_{1}/\theta_{1})
for
({z}_{1},{z}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then, since
\mathbb{A}_{p}
and
\rho_{p}
are
1
–periodic in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–direction, one can expect
U
to be also
1
–periodic in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–direction, that is
U(\cdot+\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{2})=U
, so that (
1.10
) reduces to a problem defined in the strip
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,1)
. This property holds if one considers, for generality, extensions
G
that are formally speaking
1
–periodic in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–direction, that is
G(\cdot+\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{2})=G
. The corresponding strip problem is studied in Section
4.1
.

(\textit{b})

Partial Floquet-Bloch transform
. In Section
4.2
, we introduce the partial Floquet-Bloch transform with respect to
{z}_{1}
. Thanks to the periodicity of
(\mathbb{A}_{p},\rho_{p})
in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{1}
–direction, this transform leads to a family of waveguide problems defined in the cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)^{2}
.

(\textit{c})

DtN approach
. At last, using the periodicity of
(\mathbb{A}^{\pm}_{p},\rho^{\pm}_{p})
in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{x}
–direction, we resort in Section
4.3
to the DtN method developed in
[
13
,
23
]
. This method allows to reduce computations to the interface
\{0\}\times(0,1)^{2}
thanks to local Dirichlet problems defined in the cells
(0,\pm 1)\times(0,1)^{2}
, and a propagation operator which satisfies a Riccati equation.

1.3
Outline of the paper

The paper is structured as follows. In Section
2
, two specific configurations of
2
D media represented by
\mathbb{A}
and
\rho
are introduced. We prove (
1.6
), namely that these
2
D media can be viewed as the restriction of
3
D augmented structures (represented by
\mathbb{A}_{p}
and
\rho_{p}
) along the hyperplane oriented along a particular matrix
{\mathbb{\Theta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 2}
. Accordingly, as explained in Section
1.2
, we extend (

P

) onto a
3
D augmented problem which is studied and solved in Section
4
using the Floquet-Bloch transform and the DtN approach developed in
[
23
]
. But beforehand, we have to dedicate Section
3
to setting up an appropriate functional framework for the analysis of the augmented 3D problem. In particular, we introduce anisotropic Sobolev spaces for which we establish trace theorems and Green’s formulas. In Section
5
, we summarize the algorithm and present its discretization. A particular attention is given to the discretization of local Dirichlet cell problems involved in the procedure. Even though these problems can be solved directly using
3
D finite elements, we exploit their anisotropic structure to instead solve a family of
2
D cell problems. This so-called quasi
2
–dimensional (or quasi-
2
D) method is inspired from the quasi-
1
D developed in
[
2
]
, although less obvious, as we demonstrate. Section
6
provides numerical results to illustrate the efficiency of the method in different situations. Finally, the present work suggests some extensions and perspectives, which we highlight in Section
7
.

Notation

•

We use the notation
\mathcal{O}_{\nu}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
{\nu}\in\{\varnothing,+,-\}
to refer to any triple of the form
\{\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}_{+},\mathcal{O}_{-}\}
, with the convention that
\mathcal{O}_{\nu}=\mathcal{O}
for
{\nu}=\varnothing
.

•

The indicator function of an open set
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is denoted by
\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

•

Given
{\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we denote by
\mathbb{Z}{\bm{p}}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}{\bm{p}}_{2}
the lattice
\mathbb{Z}{\bm{p}}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}{\bm{p}}_{2}:=\{n_{1}\,{\bm{p}}_{1}+n_{2}\,{\bm{p}}_{2}\ /\ n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. Similarly, for
\textnormal{{p}}_{1},\textnormal{{p}}_{2},\textnormal{{p}}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, let
\mathbb{Z}\textnormal{{p}}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}\textnormal{{p}}_{2}+\mathbb{Z}\textnormal{{p}}_{3}:=\{n_{1}\,\textnormal{{p}}_{1}+n_{2}\,\textnormal{{p}}_{2}+n_{3}\,\textnormal{{p}}_{3}\ /\ n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

•

\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\mathbb{A}}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
denotes the transpose of
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times m}
. The
n\times n
identity matrix is denoted by
\mathbb{I}_{n}
.

•

For
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the scalar product on
L^{2}(\mathcal{O})
is denoted by
(\cdot,\cdot)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O})}
. We denote by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\partial\mathcal{O}}
the duality product between
H^{-1/2}(\partial\mathcal{O})
and
H^{1/2}(\partial\mathcal{O})
.

•

Given two Banach spaces
\mathscr{X}_{1}
and
\mathscr{X}_{2}
, the space of bounded linear operators from
\mathscr{X}_{1}
to
\mathscr{X}_{2}
is denoted by
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}_{1},\mathscr{X}_{2})
. We set
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X}):=\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{X})
.

2
The model configurations and their hidden quasiperiodic nature

In this section, we present the properties of the functions
\mathbb{A}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}),\rho\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
involved in (

P

), and we highlight their quasiperiodic nature as restrictions of
3
D functions along a hyperplane.

2.1
Two specific configurations

We assume that
\mathbb{A}
and
\rho
can be written as:

\displaystyle\operatorname{a.e.}\;{\bm{x}}=({x},{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\mathbb{A}({\bm{x}}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad\textnormal{if}\ \ }l}\mathbb{A}^{-}({\bm{x}})&{x}<0,\\[6.0pt]
\mathbb{A}^{+}({\bm{x}})&{x}>0,\end{array}\right.\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\rho({\bm{x}}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad\textnormal{if}\ \ }l}\rho^{-}({\bm{x}})&{x}<0,\\[6.0pt]
\rho^{+}({\bm{x}})&{x}>0,\end{array}\right.

(2.1)

where
\mathbb{A}^{\pm}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})
,
\rho^{\pm}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. We consider two specific classes of functions
(\mathbb{A}^{\pm},\rho^{\pm})
(see Section
7.2
for the extension to more general cases).

\mathscr{A}
.

Configuration (

A

) —
The media are periodic along the interface
\sigma=\{{x}=0\}

This setting consists in
\mathbb{A}^{\pm}
and
\rho^{\pm}
being
\mathbb{Z}{\bm{e}}_{x}+\mathbb{Z}(p^{\pm}_{z}{\bm{e}}_{z})
–periodic for some numbers
p^{\pm}_{z}>0
, that is,

\forall\;{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l@{\quad\textnormal{and} \quad}r@{\ =\ }l}\mathbb{A}^{\pm}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}_{x})&\mathbb{A}^{\pm}({\bm{x}}),&\mathbb{A}^{\pm}({\bm{x}}+p^{\pm}_{z}{\bm{e}}_{z})&\mathbb{A}^{\pm}({\bm{x}}),\\[8.0pt]
\rho^{\pm}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}_{x})&\rho^{\pm}({\bm{x}}),&\rho^{\pm}({\bm{x}}+p^{\pm}_{z}{\bm{e}}_{z})&\rho^{\pm}({\bm{x}}).\end{array}\right.

(2.2)

This is illustrated in Figure
3
.

Remark 2.1
.

(\textit{a})
.

If the ratio
p^{+}_{z}/p^{-}_{z}
is a rational number that can be written as
a/b
for some coprime
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{N}^{*}
, then
(\mathbb{A}^{+},\rho^{+})
and
(\mathbb{A}^{-},\rho^{-})
share a common period
\tau:=b\,p^{+}_{z}=a\,p^{-}_{z}
in the
{\bm{e}}_{z}
–direction. It follows that the overall medium represented by
(\mathbb{A},\rho)
is periodic in the
{\bm{e}}_{z}
–direction. Hence, as done in
[
12
]
, a Floquet-Bloch transform can be applied with respect to the variable
{z}
along the interface, reducing (

P

) to a family of waveguide problems set in
\mathbb{R}\times(0,\tau)
, and parameterized by the Floquet variable. However, this method becomes more costly as the denominator of
p^{+}_{z}/p^{-}_{z}
, and thus the period
\tau
increases. It could then be relevant to use, in this case, the method described in this paper.

(\textit{b})
.

When
p^{+}_{z}/p^{-}_{z}
is irrational, the method in
[
12
]
cannot be applied directly because
\mathbb{A}
and
\rho
are no longer periodic along the interface. In this case, one might be tempted to construct a rational approximation
(a_{n}/b_{n})_{n}
of
p^{+}_{z}/p^{-}_{z}
, and to compute, using
[
12
]
, the solution
u_{n}
of (

P

), obtained by replacing
p^{+}_{z}/p^{-}_{z}
by
a_{n}/b_{n}
for
n
large enough. However, in addition to the theoretical questions that such a strategy raises (regarding for instance the convergence of
(u_{n})_{n}
to
u
), there are some numerical drawbacks. In fact, for a sequence of rationals
(a_{n}/b_{n})_{n}
to converge to an irrational
p^{+}_{z}/p^{-}_{z}
, the sequence of denominators
(b_{n})_{n}
must tend to infinity. Therefore, as explained in the first point, computational costs would inevitably increase with the periods
(\tau_{n})_{n}
as
(a_{n}/b_{n})_{n}
tends to
p^{+}_{z}/p^{-}_{z}
. Furthermore, the approximation quality would be strongly related to the irrationality measure of
p^{+}_{z}/p^{-}_{z}
, which indicates how efficiently it can be approximated by rational numbers. More detail about this aspect on rational approximation can be found in
[
19
, Chapter XI]
.

Figure 3
:
Configuration (

A

): Juxtaposition of two media that are periodic along the interface

\mathscr{B}
.

Configuration (

B

) — Junction of a homogeneous medium and a periodic one

This corresponds to the case where
\mathbb{A}^{-}
and
\rho^{-}
are constant while
\mathbb{A}^{+}
and
\rho^{+}
are
\mathbb{Z}{\bm{e}}_{x}+\mathbb{Z}{\bm{p}}^{+}
–periodic for some vector
{\bm{p}}^{+}=(p^{+}_{x},p^{+}_{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
p^{+}_{z}\neq 0
: for
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\forall\;{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ \equiv\ }l@{\quad\textnormal{and} \quad}r@{\ =\ }l@{, \quad}r@{\ =\ }l}\mathbb{A}^{-}({\bm{x}})&\mathbb{A}^{-}&\mathbb{A}^{+}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}_{x})&\mathbb{A}^{+}({\bm{x}})&\mathbb{A}^{+}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{p}}^{+})&\mathbb{A}^{+}({\bm{x}}),\\[8.0pt]
\rho^{-}({\bm{x}})&\rho^{-}&\rho^{+}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}_{x})&\rho^{+}({\bm{x}})&\rho^{+}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{p}}^{+})&\rho^{+}({\bm{x}}),\end{array}\right.

(2.3)

as illustrated in Figure
4
.

Remark 2.2
.

Note that
p^{+}_{z}{\bm{e}}_{z}={\bm{p}}^{+}-p^{+}_{x}{\bm{e}}_{x}
. Therefore if
p^{+}_{x}
is rational with the irreducible form
a/b
,
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{N}^{*}
, then
b\,p^{+}_{z}{\bm{e}}_{z}=b\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{p}}^{+}-a\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{e}}_{x}
and thus
(\mathbb{A}^{+},\rho^{+})
are
\tau
–periodic in the
{\bm{e}}_{z}
–direction with
\tau:=b\,p^{+}_{z}
. Conversely, if
p^{+}_{x}
is irrational, then the medium is no longer periodic in the direction of the interface. In that regard, Remark
2.1
translates to Configuration (

B

) when
p^{+}_{z}/p^{-}_{z}
is replaced by
p^{+}_{x}
.

Figure 4
:
Configuration (

B

): Juxtaposition of a periodic medium and a homogeneous one

2.2
A hidden quasiperiodicity along the interface

The goal of this section is to show that the functions
\mathbb{A}
and
\rho
given by Configurations (

A

) and (

B

) can be viewed as quasiperiodic along the interface
\sigma
, in the sense that they are restrictions to a hyperplane of
3
D functions that are periodic along a
2
D interface containing
\sigma
. More precisely, we shall prove (
1.6
), which is recalled below:

\operatorname{a.e.}\;{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\mathbb{A}({\bm{x}})=\mathbb{A}_{p}({\mathbb{\Theta}}\,{\bm{x}})\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\rho({\bm{x}})=\rho_{p}({\mathbb{\Theta}}\,{\bm{x}}),

(2.4a)

where
\mathbb{A}_{p}
and
\rho_{p}
are defined by

\displaystyle\forall\;\textnormal{{x}}=({x},{z}_{1},{z}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\quad\mathbb{A}_{p}(\textnormal{{x}}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{,\quad}l}\mathbb{A}^{+}_{p}(\textnormal{{x}})&x>0\\[6.0pt]
\mathbb{A}^{-}_{p}(\textnormal{{x}})&x<0\end{array}\right.\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\rho_{p}(\textnormal{{x}}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{,\quad}l}\rho^{+}_{p}(\textnormal{{x}})&x>0\\[6.0pt]
\rho^{-}_{p}(\textnormal{{x}})&x<0,\end{array}\right.

(2.4b)

with
\mathbb{A}^{\pm}_{p},\rho^{\pm}_{p}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
which are
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
–periodic. Furthermore, the matrix
{\mathbb{\Theta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 2}
, which will be referred to as the
cut matrix
, has the following form:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{\Theta}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&\theta_{1}\\
0&\theta_{2}\end{pmatrix}\quad\textnormal{with}\quad\theta_{1}\neq 0,

(2.4c)

the exact expression of
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
depending on the configuration.

2.2.1
Quasiperiodicity of Configuration (

A

)

To find
(\mathbb{A}_{p},\rho_{p})
, the formal idea is to split the tangential variable
{z}
into two variables
{z}_{1}
and
{z}_{2}
, where
{z}_{1}
is associated to the periodicity of the medium in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
, and where
{z}_{2}
is associated to the periodicity of the medium in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-}
. In addition, to ensure that the medium is
1
–periodic with respect to
{z}_{1}
and
{z}_{2}
, this splitting comes with a rescaling:
{z}_{1}
will correspond to
{z}/p^{+}_{z}
, while
{z}_{2}
will correspond to
{z}/p^{-}_{z}
. This suggests to introduce

\displaystyle\forall\;\textnormal{{x}}=({x},{z}_{1},{z}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ :=\ }l@{\qquad\textnormal{and} \qquad}r@{\ :=\ }l}\mathbb{A}^{+}_{p}(\textnormal{{x}})&\mathbb{A}^{+}({x},\mskip 1.5mup^{+}_{z}\,{z}_{1})&\rho^{+}_{p}(\textnormal{{x}})&\rho^{+}({x},\mskip 1.5mup^{+}_{z}\,{z}_{1})\\[10.0pt]
\mathbb{A}^{-}_{p}(\textnormal{{x}})&\mathbb{A}^{-}({x},\mskip 1.5mup^{-}_{z}\,{z}_{2})&\rho^{-}_{p}(\textnormal{{x}})&\rho^{-}({x},\mskip 1.5mup^{-}_{z}\,{z}_{2}),\end{array}\right.

(2.5)

where
(\mathbb{A}^{+}_{p},\rho^{+}_{p})
are
1
–periodic in
{x}
,
1
–periodic in
{z}_{1}
, and independent of
{z}_{2}
, while
(\mathbb{A}^{-}_{p},\rho^{-}_{p})
are
1
–periodic in
{x}
, independent of
{z}_{1}
, and
1
–periodic in
{z}_{2}
, as illustrated in Figure
5
.

Now let
(\mathbb{A}_{p},\rho_{p})
be given by (
2.4b
) using
(\mathbb{A}^{\pm}_{p},\rho^{\pm}_{p})
. Then, since both
(\mathbb{A}^{+}_{p},\rho^{+}_{p})
and
(\mathbb{A}^{-}_{p},\rho^{-}_{p})
are
1
–periodic with respect to
{z}_{1}
and
{z}_{2}
, it follows that
(\mathbb{A}_{p},\rho_{p})
are periodic along the interface
\Sigma
.

Figure 5
:
Left: Schematic representation of the augmented structure for the Configuration (

A

)–type medium represented in Figure
3
. Right: Close-up view of a periodicity cell.

Moreover, by
“inverting”
(
2.5
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\forall\;{\bm{x}}=({x},{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\mathbb{A}^{\pm}({\bm{x}})=\mathbb{A}^{\pm}_{p}({x},\mskip 1.5mu{z}/p^{+}_{z},\mskip 1.5mu{z}/p^{-}_{z})\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\rho^{\pm}({\bm{x}})=\rho^{\pm}_{p}({x},\mskip 1.5mu{z}/p^{+}_{z},\mskip 1.5mu{z}/p^{-}_{z}).

(2.6)

This corresponds to (
2.4a
) and (
2.4c
) with

\displaystyle\theta_{1}:=1/p^{+}_{z}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\theta_{2}:=1/p^{-}_{z}.

(2.7)

Finally, note that the half-plane
{\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\pm}
(
resp.
the line
{\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\sigma
) is included in the half-space
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm}
(
resp.
the interface
\Sigma
defined by (
1.7
)).

2.2.2
Quasiperiodicity of Configuration (

B

)

From its properties (
2.3
), we shall see that
\mathbb{A}^{+}
admits the expression (
2.4a
), although it is less obvious than in Configuration (

A

). It is useful to
“look at
\mathbb{A}^{+}
in the basis
\{{\bm{e}}_{x},{\bm{p}}^{+}\}
”
by defining

\forall\;(\accentset{\circ}{{x}},\accentset{\circ}{{z}})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\accentset{\circ}{\mathbb{A}}^{+}(\accentset{\circ}{{x}},\accentset{\circ}{{z}}):=\mathbb{A}^{+}(\accentset{\circ}{{x}}\,{\bm{e}}_{x}+\accentset{\circ}{{z}}\,{\bm{p}}^{+}):=\mathbb{A}^{+}(\accentset{\circ}{{x}}+\accentset{\circ}{{z}}\,p^{+}_{x},\,\accentset{\circ}{{z}}\,p^{+}_{z}).

(2.8)

From the periodicity properties (
2.3
) of
\mathbb{A}^{+}
it follows that
\accentset{\circ}{\mathbb{A}}^{+}
is 1–periodic with respect to its variables. Moreover, considering the change of variables

\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l}\accentset{\circ}{{x}}+p^{+}_{x}\accentset{\circ}{{z}}&{x}\\[5.0pt]
p^{+}_{z}\accentset{\circ}{{z}}&{z}\end{array}\right\}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l}\accentset{\circ}{{x}}&{x}-(p^{+}_{x}/p^{+}_{z})\,{z}\\[5.0pt]
\accentset{\circ}{{z}}&{z}/p^{+}_{z},\end{array}\right.

which is well-defined because
p^{+}_{z}\neq 0
, (
2.8
) can be
“inverted”
as

\forall\;{\bm{x}}=({x},{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\mathbb{A}^{+}({\bm{x}})=\accentset{\circ}{\mathbb{A}}^{+}({x}-(p^{+}_{x}/p^{+}_{z})\,{z},{z}/p^{+}_{z}).

(2.9)

Thus, similarly to Configuration (

A

), we split the tangential variable
{z}
into a variable
{z}_{1}
corresponding to
{z}/p^{+}_{z}
, and a variable
{z}_{2}
corresponding to
-(p^{+}_{x}/p^{+}_{z})\,{z}
. Accordingly, it is natural to define the function

\displaystyle\forall\;\textnormal{{x}}=({x},{z}_{1},{z}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\quad\mathbb{A}^{+}_{p}(\textnormal{{x}}):=\accentset{\circ}{\mathbb{A}}^{+}(x+{z}_{2},{z}_{1}),

(2.10)

where
\mathbb{A}^{+}_{p}
is
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
–periodic, and in particular periodic with respect to the variables
({z}_{1},{z}_{2})
of the interface
\Sigma
defined by (
1.7
), as illustrated in Figure
6
. Then, (
2.9
) becomes

\displaystyle\forall\;{\bm{x}}=({x},{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\mathbb{A}^{+}({\bm{x}})=\mathbb{A}^{+}_{p}({x},{z}/p^{+}_{z},-(p^{+}_{x}/p^{+}_{z})\,{z}).

(2.11)

The same arguments can be applied to
\rho
, to define
\rho^{+}_{p}
similarly to (
2.10
). In addition,
\mathbb{A}^{-}
and
\rho^{-}
are extended as constant functions over
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. More precisely, we set

\forall\;\textnormal{{x}}=({x},{z}_{1},{z}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\quad\displaystyle\mathbb{A}^{-}_{p}(\textnormal{{x}}):=\mathbb{A}^{-}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\rho^{-}_{p}(\textnormal{{x}}):=\rho^{-},

(2.12)

and we define
\mathbb{A}_{p}
and
\rho_{p}
as in (
2.4b
), so that (
2.4a
) and (
2.4c
) hold with

\displaystyle\theta_{1}:=1/p^{+}_{z}\neq 0\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\theta_{2}:=-p^{+}_{x}/p^{+}_{z}.

(2.13)

Finally, note that the half-plane
{\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\pm}
(
resp.
the line
{\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\sigma
) is included in the half-space
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm}
(
resp.
the interface
\Sigma
).

Figure 6
:
Left: Schematic representation of the augmented structure for the Configuration (

B

)–type medium represented in Figure
4
(some cylinders have been made more transparent for readability). Right: Close-up view of a periodicity cell.

3
Functional framework

The formal procedure described in Section
1.2
consists in seeking the solution
u
of (

P

) under the form
u({\bm{x}})=U({\mathbb{\Theta}}{\bm{x}})
, where
U
satisfies

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l}\displaystyle-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\bm{\nabla}}{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\bm{\nabla}\,U-\rho_{p}\,\omega^{2}\,U&0\quad\textnormal{in}\ \ \mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\cup\mathbb{R}^{3}_{-},\\[12.0pt]
\llbracket({\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\bm{\nabla}\,U)\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{x}\rrbracket_{\Sigma}&G.\end{array}\right.

(3.1)

The advantage of the augmented equation (
3.1
) lies in its periodic nature, which allows to use adapted tools for periodic PDEs, such as the Floquet-Bloch transform. Nevertheless, a difficulty is that the differential operator
-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\bm{\nabla}}{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\bm{\nabla}
is
elliptically degenerate
because the matrix
{\mathbb{\Theta}}
given by (
2.4c
) is of rank
2
. More precisely,

\displaystyle\operatorname{a.e.}\;{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\Ker\big[{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}({\bm{x}})\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\big]=\vect\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-\theta_{2}\\
\phantom{-}\theta_{1}\end{pmatrix}.

As a consequence, the properties of (
3.1
) differ substantially from those of the classical Helmholtz equation given by
-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\bm{\nabla}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}\mskip 1.5mu\bm{\nabla}U-\rho\,\omega^{2}\,U=0
. In particular, one needs an appropriate functional framework to take the anisotropic nature of (
3.1
) into account. This is the object of this section, which sets up the framework that will be used afterwards.

3.1
Anisotropic Sobolev spaces: motivation and outline

To begin, let us define for any open set
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
the anisotropic Sobolev spaces

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r@{\ :=\ }l}H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathcal{O})&\{U\in L^{2}(\mathcal{O})\ /\ \mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\bm{\nabla}U\in[L^{2}(\mathcal{O})]^{2}\},\\[15.0pt]
H_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathbf{div};\mathcal{O})&\{\bm{W}\in[L^{2}(\mathcal{O})]^{2}\ /\ \mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\bm{\nabla}}{\mathbb{\Theta}}\bm{W}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{O})\}.\end{array}

(3.2)

These are Hilbert spaces when equipped with the respective scalar products

\begin{array}[]{c@{\quad\ }r@{\ :=\ }l}\forall\;U,V\in H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathcal{O}),&\displaystyle(U,V)_{H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathcal{O})}&\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{O}}\big[\mskip 1.5muU\,\overline{V}+\big(\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\bm{\nabla}U\big)\cdot\big(\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\bm{\nabla}\overline{V}\big)\mskip 1.5mu\big],\\[15.0pt]
\displaystyle\forall\;\bm{W},\widetilde{\bm{W}}\in H_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathbf{div};\mathcal{O}),&\displaystyle(\bm{W},\widetilde{\bm{W}})_{H_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathbf{div};\mathcal{O})}&\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{O}}\big[\mskip 1.5mu\bm{W}\cdot\overline{\widetilde{\bm{W}}}+(\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\bm{\nabla}}{\mathbb{\Theta}}\bm{W})\;(\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\bm{\nabla}}{\mathbb{\Theta}}\overline{\widetilde{\bm{W}}})\mskip 1.5mu\big].\end{array}

We shall denote by
\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathcal{O})}
and
\|\cdot\|_{H_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathbf{div};\mathcal{O})}
the respective induced norms.

Due to the domains introduced throughout the paper, a specific attention will be given to
“rectangle”
based cylindrical domains in what follows. Let
I_{x},I_{1}
be intervals which do not need to be bounded, and such that
0\in\overline{I_{x}}
. We consider the
3
–dimensional domain
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}
and the
2
–dimensional transverse set
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}
given by

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r@{\ :=\ \big\{(\xvi, \zvi_1, \zvi_2) \in\R^3\ /\ }c@{\ \ \zvi_1 \in I_1,\ \ \zvi_2 \in\R}l@{\quad\equiv\ }l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}\ :=\ \big\{({x},{z}_{1},{z}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}&{x}\in I_{x},\hfil\ \ {z}_{1}\in I_{1},\ \ {z}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}&\big\}&I_{x}\times I_{1}\times\mathbb{R},\\[10.0pt]
\forall\;\tau\in\overline{I_{x}},\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}\ :=\ \big\{({x},{z}_{1},{z}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}&{x}=\tau,\hfil\ \ {z}_{1}\in I_{1},\ \ {z}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}&\big\}&\{\tau\}\times I_{1}\times\mathbb{R},\end{array}

(3.3)

with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}=:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}
for
\tau=0
. The above domains are unbounded in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–direction. We will be interested in the
“
{z}_{2}
–cell domains”
, bounded in
{z}_{2}
:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r@{\ :=\ \big\{(\xvi, \zvi_1, \zvi_2)\ }c@{\ /\ \zvi_2 \in(0, 1)}l@{\quad\equiv\ }l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}\ :=\ \big\{({x},{z}_{1},{z}_{2}&\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}\hfil\ /\ {z}_{2}&\big\}&I_{x}\times I_{1}\times(0,1),\\[10.0pt]
\forall\;\tau\in\overline{I_{x}},\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}\ :=\ \big\{({x},{z}_{1},{z}_{2}&\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}\hfil\ /\ {z}_{2}&\big\}&\{\tau\}\times I_{1}\times(0,1),\end{array}

(3.4)

with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}=:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
for
\tau=0
. The subscript
“

\textstyle\#

”
refers to the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
are bounded in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–direction. However, note that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
may be bounded or unbounded in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{1}
–direction, depending on whether
I_{1}
is bounded or not. The same applies to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{x}
–direction. Figure
7
(left) represents these domains for
I_{x}=\mathbb{R}_{-}
and
I_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
{x}
{z}_{1}
\smash{{z}_{2}}
\tau
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
I_{1}
Q
{x}
{z}
\widehat{I}

Figure 7
:
Left: the rectangle-based cylindrical domain
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
and the lateral set
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
given by (
3.4
). Right: the domains
Q
and
\widehat{I}
given by (
3.8
).
I_{x}=\mathbb{R}_{-}
and
I_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

For smooth functions
V\in\mathscr{C}^{1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
,
\bm{W}\in[\mathscr{C}^{1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})]^{2}
, one has the Green’s formula

\displaystyle\int_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\big[(\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\bm{\nabla}}{\mathbb{\Theta}}\bm{W})\,\overline{V}+\bm{W}\cdot\overline{\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,\bm{\nabla}V}\big]=\int_{\partial\mskip 1.5mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}({\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\bm{W}\cdot\textnormal{{n}})\,\overline{V},

where
n
denotes the unit normal vector on
\partial\mskip 1.5mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
which is outward with respect to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
. In what follows, we shall be more interested in two cases:

(\textnormal{C}.1)

The strip
:
I_{1}=\mathbb{R}
and
V,\bm{W}
are
1
–periodic with respect to
{z}_{2}
;

(\textnormal{C}.2)

The cylinder
:
I_{1}=(0,1)
and
V,\bm{W}
are
1
–periodic with respect to
{z}_{1}
and
{z}_{2}
.

In both cases, the above Green’s formula becomes

\displaystyle\int_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\big[(\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\bm{\nabla}}{\mathbb{\Theta}}\bm{W})\,\overline{V}+\bm{W}\cdot\overline{\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,\bm{\nabla}V}\big]=\sum_{\tau\in\partial I_{x}}\int_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}({\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\bm{W}\cdot\textnormal{{n}})\,\overline{V},

(3.5)

where the right-hand side is reduced to the surface integral on the transverse faces
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
,
\tau\in\partial I_{x}
. Our goal is to extend the above formula to
(V,\bm{W})\in H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})\times H_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathbf{div};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
(that is, to prove (
3.26
) and (
3.33
) for the cases (

⁢
C
.1

) and (

⁢
C
.2

) defined above respectively). This requires to define properly the traces
V|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}
(Section
3.3
) and
({\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\bm{W}\cdot\textnormal{{n}})|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}
(Section
3.4
), and to replace the integral in the right-hand side of (
3.5
) by a duality product between the appropriate trace spaces (Section
3.4
for Case (

⁢
C
.1

) and Section
3.5
for Case (

⁢
C
.2

)).

As a first step, it is useful to introduce a so-called shear map (see (
3.13
)), to charaterize any function in
H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
(
resp.
in
H_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathbf{div};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
) which is periodic with respect to
{z}_{2}
in terms of its
L^{2}
–regularity in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–direction and its
H^{1}
–regularity in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{x}
and
\theta_{1}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1}+\theta_{2}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–directions. This is the object of the next section.

3.2
Anisotropic spaces of
\mathbb{Z}\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–periodic functions

Given numbers
-\infty\leq a<b\leq+\infty
and a Banach space
(\mathscr{X},\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{X}})
, we recall that

L^{2}(a,b;\mathscr{X}):=\displaystyle\Big\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bm{V}}\ /\ \ \|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bm{V}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(a,b;\mathscr{X})}:=\int_{a}^{b}\|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bm{V}}(\cdot,s)\|^{2}_{\mathscr{X}}\;ds<+\infty\Big\}.

(3.6)

Our starting point is the oblique transformation

\displaystyle\operatorname{T}_{\theta}:({\bm{x}},s)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\ \longmapsto\ {\mathbb{\Theta}}\,{\bm{x}}+s\,\textnormal{{e}}_{2}=({x},\theta_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{z},\theta_{2}\mskip 1.5mu{z}+s)\in\mathbb{R}^{3},

(3.7)

which relates the
3
D operator
\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,\bm{\nabla}
to the
2
D gradient
\nabla
by means of the chain rule (
1.9
) recalled as
\nabla[F\circ\operatorname{T}_{\theta}(\cdot,s)]({\bm{x}})=(\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,\bm{\nabla}F)\circ\operatorname{T}_{\theta}({\bm{x}},s)
, for any
\mathscr{C}^{1}
function
F
. Consider the
2
D domain
Q
and the edge
\widehat{I}^{\tau}
given by

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{r@{\ :=\ \big\{(\xvi, \zvi) \in\R^2\ /\ }c@{\ \ \cuti_1\vts\zvi\in I_1}l}Q\ :=\ \big\{({x},{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}&{x}\in I_{x},\hfil\ \ \theta_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{z}\in I_{1}&\big\},\\[10.0pt]
\widehat{I}^{\tau}\ :=\ \big\{({x},{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}&{x}=\tau,\hfil\ \ \theta_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{z}\in I_{1}&\big\},\end{array}

(3.8)

with
\widehat{I}^{\tau}=:\widehat{I}
for
\tau=0
(see Figure
7
(right) for
I_{x}=\mathbb{R}_{-}
and
I_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
). We note that
Q
and
\widehat{I}^{\tau}
are well-defined, since for Configurations (

A

) and (

B

), we have
\theta_{1}\neq 0
. Moreover,
\operatorname{T}_{\theta}
is bijective from
Q\times\mathbb{R}
(
resp.

\widehat{I}^{\tau}\times\mathbb{R}
) to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}
(
resp.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}
). The image of
Q\times(0,1)
(
resp.

\widehat{I}^{\tau}\times(0,1)
) by
\operatorname{T}_{\theta}
is the oblique domain
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta}
(
resp.
the oblique face
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta}
) represented in Figure
8(b)
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta}:=\operatorname{T}_{\theta}\big(Q\times(0,1)\big)\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta}:=\operatorname{T}_{\theta}\big(\widehat{I}^{\tau}\times(0,1)\big),

(3.9)

with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}^{\tau}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta}=:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta}
for
\tau=0
.

As a preliminary step, one can use the change of variables
({\bm{x}},s)\mapsto\operatorname{T}_{\theta}({\bm{x}},s)
and Fubini’s theorem, to prove the charactization

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l}H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta})&\Big\{V\in L^{2}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta})\ /\ V\circ\operatorname{T}_{\theta}\in L^{2}\big(\mathbb{R};H^{1}(Q)\big)\Big\},\\[15.0pt]
H_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathbf{div};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta})&\Big\{\bm{W}\in[L^{2}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta})]^{2}\ /\ \bm{W}\circ\operatorname{T}_{\theta}\in L^{2}\big(\mathbb{R};H(\mathop{{\hbox{\rm div}}}\nolimits;Q)\big)\Big\},\end{array}

(3.10)

which is useful to derive the properties of
H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta})
and
H_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathbf{div};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta})
from the well-studied spaces
L^{2}(\mathbb{R};H^{1}(Q))
,
L^{2}(\mathbb{R};H(\mathop{{\hbox{\rm div}}}\nolimits;Q))
. We would like to provide similar expressions for the spaces of
\mathbb{Z}\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–periodic
H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}
or
H_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathbf{div})
functions defined on the straight domain
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
(instead of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta}
). To this end, we shall, roughly speaking, identify functions on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta}
, using the notion of
periodic extension in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–direction
. In the sequel, we fix
d\in\{1,2\}
, and we use the notation
\bm{V}
(in bold) to refer to a
\mathbb{C}^{d}
–valued function.

(a)

{x}
{z}
s
\widehat{I}
Q

\operatorname{T}_{\theta}

(b)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta}
{x}
{z}_{1}
\smash{{z}_{2}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta}
I_{1}

(a) The strip
Q\times(0,1)
, where
Q
is the hatched domain defined by (
3.8
). (b) The oblique domains
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta}
defined by (
3.9
). The hatched domain is
\operatorname{T}_{\theta}(Q\times\{0\})
.
Any function in
H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta})
or
H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
is
L^{2}
in the

direction, and

in the

and
\theta_{1}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1}+\theta_{2}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–directions indicated by the dashed arrows. (c) The point
\operatorname{T}_{\theta}({\bm{x}},s)
defined by (
3.7
) for

and for

. We fix

and

.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\theta}

Definition 3.1

(Periodic extension)
.

Let
p>0
and
\bm{V}\in[L^{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})]^{d}
. The
periodic extension of
\bm{V}
in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–direction
is the function
\mathrm{E}^{2}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5mu\bm{V}\in[L^{p}_{\textit{loc}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}})]^{d}
defined by:

\operatorname{a.e.}\;\textnormal{{x}}=({x},{z}_{1},{z}_{2})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\quad\forall\;n\in\mathbb{Z},\quad\mathrm{E}^{2}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5mu\bm{V}(\textnormal{{x}}+n\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{2}):=\bm{V}(\textnormal{{x}}).

(3.11)

For any
\Phi\in L^{p}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
, we define
\mathrm{E}^{2}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5mu\Phi\in L^{p}_{\textit{loc}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}})
similarly, by replacing
\textnormal{{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
in (
3.11
) by
\textnormal{{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{\Gamma}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}
.

Let
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{0,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}})
denote the space of smooth functions in
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}
that are compactly supported in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{x}
and
\textnormal{{e}}_{1}
–directions, and
1
–periodic in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–direction, that is,

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{0,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}):=\big\{V\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{0}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}})\ /\ \mathrm{E}^{2}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5muV\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}})\big\}.

(3.12)

Note that
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{0,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}})
contains
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
, and hence, is dense in
L^{2}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
.

The change of variables (
3.7
) combined with the periodic extension along
\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
in Definition
3.1
allows us to introduce the so-called shear transform defined by

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ } c@{\ }c@{\ }l}{\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}:&[\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{0,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}})]^{d}&\longrightarrow&[\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(Q\times\mathbb{R})]^{d}\\[5.0pt]
&\bm{V}&\longmapsto&\bm{V}_{\mathbb{\Theta}},\end{array}\quad\bm{V}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}({\bm{x}},s):=(\mathrm{E}^{2}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5mu\bm{V})\circ\operatorname{T}_{\theta}({\bm{x}},s),\quad\forall\;({\bm{x}},s)\in Q\times\mathbb{R}.

(3.13)

The definition of
\mathrm{E}^{2}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5mu
implies that
{\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}\bm{V}(\cdot,s+1)={\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}\bm{V}(\cdot,s)
. For this reason, the study of
{\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}
will be restricted to
s\in(0,1)
. As stated in the next proposition proved in Appendix
A.1
,
{\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}
extends by density to
L^{2}
–functions.

Proposition 3.2
.

The mapping
{\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}
defined by (
3.13
) extends by density to a mapping defined from
[L^{2}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})]^{d}
to
L^{2}(0,1;[L^{2}(Q)]^{d})
, with

\displaystyle\forall\;\bm{U},\bm{V}\in[L^{2}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})]^{d},\quad\frac{1}{\theta_{1}}\,\int_{0}^{1}\int_{Q}{\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}\bm{U}({\bm{x}},s)\cdot\overline{{\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}\bm{V}({\bm{x}},s)}\;d{\bm{x}}ds=\int_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\bm{U}\cdot\overline{\bm{V}}.

(3.14)

Moreover,
{\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}
is an isomorphism from
[L^{2}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})]^{d}
to
L^{2}(0,1;[L^{2}(Q)]^{d})
, and its inverse is given for any
\bm{V}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}\in L^{2}(0,1;[L^{2}(Q)]^{d})
by

\displaystyle\operatorname{a.e.}\;\textnormal{{x}}=({x},{z}_{1},{z}_{2})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\quad{\mathscr{S}^{-1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}}{\bm{V}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}}(\textnormal{{x}})=\sqrt{\theta_{1}}\;\mathrm{E}^{s}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5mu\bm{V}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}\big({x},\,{z}_{1}/\theta_{1},\,{z}_{2}-{z}_{1}(\theta_{2}/\theta_{1})\big),

(3.15)

where
\mathrm{E}^{s}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5mu\bm{V}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}\in[L^{2}_{\textit{loc}}(Q\times\mathbb{R})]^{d}
denotes the periodic extension of
\bm{V}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}
with respect to the variable
s
, defined for almost any
({\bm{x}},s)\in Q\times(0,1)
and for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
by
\mathrm{E}^{s}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5mu\bm{V}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}({\bm{x}},s+n):=\bm{V}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}({\bm{x}},s)
.

Now, by analogy with (
3.10
), we define

\displaystyle\boxed{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ :=\ }l}H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})&\big\{V\in L^{2}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})\ /\ {\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}V\in L^{2}(0,1;H^{1}(Q))\big\},\\[15.0pt]
H_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\mathbf{div};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})&\big\{\bm{W}\in[L^{2}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})]^{2}\ /\ {\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}\bm{W}\in L^{2}(0,1;H(\mathop{{\hbox{\rm div}}}\nolimits;Q))\big\}.\end{array}}

(3.16)

It is not obvious from their definition that
H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
and
H_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\mathbf{div};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
are respectively subspaces of
H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
and
H_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathbf{div};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
. This is however true from the chain rule, as highlighted in the next proposition proved in Appendix
A.2
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Any
V\in H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
belongs to
H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
and

\displaystyle\operatorname{a.e.}\;({\bm{x}},s)\in Q\times(0,1),\quad{\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}(\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\bm{\nabla}V)({\bm{x}},s)=\nabla_{\bm{x}}({\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}V)({\bm{x}},s).

(3.17)

Similarly, any
\bm{W}\in H_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\mathbf{div};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
belongs to
H_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathbf{div};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
and

\displaystyle\operatorname{a.e.}\;({\bm{x}},s)\in Q\times(0,1),\quad{\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}(\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\bm{\nabla}}{\mathbb{\Theta}}\bm{W})({\bm{x}},s)=\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\nabla}_{\bm{x}}({\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}\bm{W})({\bm{x}},s),

(3.18)

where
\nabla_{\bm{x}}
is the gradient operator with respect to
{\bm{x}}
.

In what follows, we equip
H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
with the scalar product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})}
, and
H_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\mathbf{div};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
with the scalar product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{H_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathbf{div};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})}
. Propositions
3.2
and
3.3
then lead to the next result.

Corollary 3.4
.

The shear map
{\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}
is an isomorphism from
H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
to
L^{2}(0,1;H^{1}(Q))
, and from
H_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\mathbf{div};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
to
L^{2}(0,1;H(\mathop{{\hbox{\rm div}}}\nolimits;Q))
. Consequently, equipped with the
H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}
(
resp
.
H_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathbf{div}))
) scalar product,
H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
(
resp
.
H_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\mathbf{div};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
) is a Hilbert space.

We finish this section with two density results which provide more insight on the nature of
H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
and
H_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\mathbf{div};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
. The proof is delayed to Appendix
A.3
.

Proposition 3.5
.

(\textit{a})
.

The space
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{0,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}})
is dense in
H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
.

(\textit{b})
.

The space
[\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{0,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}})]^{2}
is dense in
H_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\mathbf{div};{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
.

Remark 3.6
.

Proposition
3.5
implies that for smooth functions, the definition of
H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})
corresponds to
1
–periodicity in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–direction. More generally, one could show the characterization

H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})=\{V\in H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{O}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})\ /\ V|_{{z}_{2}=0}=V|_{{z}_{2}=1}\},

even though the equality in this last characterization has to be understood not in the
L^{2}
sense, but in the
L^{2}_{\textit{loc}}
sense. In fact, by adapting the results in
[
2
, Section 3.2.1]
, it can be shown that the space of traces on
\{{z}_{2}=a\}
involves a weighted
L^{2}
space.

3.3
Trace operator on transverse interfaces
