3.32
), its dual
\smash{H^{-1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma^{n}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})}
, and the trace and the normal trace applications on
\Sigma^{n}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
. In the sequel, we will systematically use the obvious identifications
H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\mathcal{C}^{n}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})\equiv H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})
and
{H^{\pm 1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma^{n}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})\equiv H^{\pm 1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})}
, even when not mentioned.

4.4.1
Structure of the half-guide solution

Consider the operator
\mathcal{P}_{k}\in\mathscr{L}(H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}))
:

\displaystyle\forall\;\Phi\in H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}),\quad\mathcal{P}_{k}\mskip 1.5mu\Phi:=\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\Phi]|_{\Sigma^{1}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}},

(4.23)

where the boundedness property stems from the well-posedness of (
\mathscr{P}^{+}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
) and from the continuity of the trace map on
\Sigma^{1}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
from
H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{+})
to
\smash{H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma^{1}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})\equiv H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})}
. The operator
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is called the
propagation operator
, because it determines how the half-guide solution propagates from one interface to the other, as the next result shows.

Proposition 4.10
.

For any
\Phi\in H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})
, the solution
\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\Phi]
of Problem
(
\mathscr{P}^{+}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
)
satisfies

\displaystyle\forall\;n\in\mathbb{N},\quad\operatorname{a.e.}\;\textnormal{{x}}\in\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}},\quad\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\Phi]\mskip 1.5mu(\textnormal{{x}}+n\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{x})=\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[(\mathcal{P}_{k})^{n}\mskip 1.5mu\Phi]\mskip 1.5mu(\textnormal{{x}}),

(4.24)

so that

\displaystyle\forall\;n\in\mathbb{N},\quad\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\Phi]|_{\Sigma^{n}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}=(\mathcal{P}_{k})^{n}\,\Phi.

(4.25)

Furthermore,
\mathcal{P}_{k}
has a spectral radius denoted by
\rho(\mathcal{P}_{k})
, which is strictly less than
1
.

Proof
.

The proof is a direct adaptation of
[
23
, Theorem 3.1]
. To prove (
4.24
), we begin with
n=1
and we define
\widetilde{U}[\Phi]:=\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\Phi]\mskip 1.5mu(\cdot+\textnormal{{e}}_{x})
. Then one has
\widetilde{U}[\Phi]|_{\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}=\mathcal{P}_{k}\mskip 1.5mu\Phi
. Moreover, by using the change of variables
\textnormal{{x}}\mapsto\textnormal{{x}}+\textnormal{{e}}_{1}
in (
\mathscr{P}^{+}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
) and the periodicity of
\mathbb{A}^{+}_{p}
and
\rho^{+}_{p}
along
\textnormal{{e}}_{x}
, one obtains that
\widetilde{U}[\Phi]
satisfies the same problem as
\smash{\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\mathcal{P}_{k}\mskip 1.5mu\Phi]}
. Consequently, the uniqueness of (
\mathscr{P}^{+}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
) leads to (
4.24
) for
n=1
. The extension to
n>1
follows by induction.

To prove that
\rho(\mathcal{P}_{k})<1
, we rely on the following estimate, which describes the exponential decay of the half-guide solution
\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}
in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{x}
–direction (see Remark
4.11
):

\exists\;c,\,\alpha>0,\quad\big\|\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\Phi]\,\exp(\alpha\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits\omega\,|{x}|)\big\|_{H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{+})}\leq c\;\|\Phi\|_{H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})}.

(4.26)

Since
\smash{(\mathcal{P}_{k})^{n}\mskip 1.5mu\Phi=\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\Phi]|_{\Sigma^{n}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}}
by (
4.24
), the continuity of the trace map on
\Sigma^{n}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
and (
4.26
) lead to

\displaystyle\exists\;c^{\prime},\alpha>0,\quad\forall\;n\in\mathbb{N},\quad\|(\mathcal{P}_{k})^{n}\|\leq c^{\prime}\;\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits\omega n}.

One then concludes using Gelfand’s formula
\displaystyle\rho(\mathcal{P}_{k})=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\|(\mathcal{P}_{k})^{n}\|^{1/n}
.

Remark 4.11
.

The proof of (
4.26
) is very similar to that of (
1.5
): the idea is to examine the problem satisfied by the product
\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}\,\exp(\beta\,|{x}|)
for any
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, and to identify the values of
\beta
for which this problem is well-posed in
H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{+})
via coercivity.

Proposition
4.10
shows that the restrictions of
\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\Phi]
to the interfaces
\Sigma^{n}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
can be fully expressed with respect to the propagation operator
\mathcal{P}_{k}
. Therefore, knowing
\mathcal{P}_{k}
, one can construct
\smash{\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\Phi]}
using solutions of problems defined in one periodicity cell, as shown in the next section.

4.4.2
Local cell problems

Given boundary data
\Phi\in H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})
and
j\in\{0,1\}
, let us introduce the local Dirichlet cell problems:
Find
E^{j}_{k}\in H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l}\displaystyle-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})}\,{\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,E^{j}_{k}-\rho_{p}\,\omega^{2}\,E^{j}_{k}&0\quad\textnormal{in}\ \ \mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}},\\[12.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\displaystyle E^{j}_{k}\in H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}),\quad\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,E^{j}_{k}\in H_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\mathbf{div};\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}),\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

(4.27a)

combined with the Dirichlet boundary conditions (see Figure
15
)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l@{\quad\textnormal{and} \quad}r@{\ =\ }l}E^{0}_{k}[\Phi]|_{\Sigma^{0}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}&\Phi&E^{0}_{k}[\Phi]|_{\Sigma^{1}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}&0\\[10.0pt]
E^{1}_{k}[\Phi]|_{\Sigma^{0}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}&0&E^{1}_{k}[\Phi]|_{\Sigma^{1}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}&\Phi.\end{array}\right.

(4.27b)

\Phi
\mathcal{T}^{00}_{k}\Phi
0
\mathcal{T}^{01}_{k}\Phi
E^{0}_{k}[\Phi]
{x}
{z}_{1}
{z}_{2}

\mathcal{T}^{10}_{k}\Phi

E^{1}_{k}[\Phi]

Figure 15
:
Dirichlet conditions satisfied by
E^{0}_{k}
and
E^{1}_{k}
, and the DtN operators
\mathcal{T}^{j\ell}_{k}
given by (
4.29
).

These problems are well-posed thanks to Lax-Milgram’s theorem combined with a lifting argument. Moreover, using the structure of
\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\Phi]
given by Proposition
4.10
, it follows by linearity that

\displaystyle\forall\;n\in\mathbb{N},\quad\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\Phi](\cdot+n\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{x})|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}=E^{0}_{k}[(\mathcal{P}_{k})^{n}\,\Phi]+E^{1}_{k}[(\mathcal{P}_{k})^{n+1}\,\Phi].

(4.28)

Therefore, if
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is known,
\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\Phi]
can be constructed cell by cell using the solutions
E^{j}_{k}[\Phi]
of the local cell problems (
4.27
). On the other hand, these local cell problems are involved in the characterization of
\mathcal{P}_{k}
. This is the object of the next section.

4.4.3
Characterization of the propagation operator via a Riccati equation

The goal of this section is to characterize
\mathcal{P}_{k}
using the
local DtN operators
defined for any
j,\ell\in\{0,1\}
by

\displaystyle\forall\;\Phi\in H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}),\quad\mathcal{T}^{j\ell}_{k}\,\Phi=(-1)^{\ell+1}\,\big({\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,\,E^{j}_{k}[\Phi]\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{x}\big)|_{\Sigma^{\ell}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}\in H^{-1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}),

(4.29)

By applying the second item of Proposition
3.16
to
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
and
\mathcal{C}^{1}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
in the cell by cell expression (
4.28
) of
\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}
, it follows that
\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{x}
is continuous accross the interface
\Sigma^{1}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
, that is,

\displaystyle\forall\;\Phi\in H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}),\quad(\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\Phi]\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{x})|_{\Sigma^{1}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}=(\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\mathcal{P}_{k}\Phi]\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{x})|_{\Sigma^{0}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}},

or equivalently, for any
\Phi\in H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})
,

\displaystyle\big(\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,E^{0}_{k}[\Phi]\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{x}\big)|_{\Sigma^{1}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}+\big(\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,E^{1}_{k}[\mathcal{P}_{k}\mskip 1.5mu\Phi]\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{x}\big)|_{\Sigma^{1}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}\\[12.0pt]
=\big(\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,E^{0}_{k}[\mathcal{P}_{k}\mskip 1.5mu\Phi]\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{x}\big)|_{\Sigma^{0}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}+\big(\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,E^{1}_{k}[(\mathcal{P}_{k})^{2}\mskip 1.5mu\Phi]\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{x}\big)|_{\Sigma^{0}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}.

(4.30)

This leads to a
Riccati
equation, which characterizes uniquely
\mathcal{P}_{k}
as stated by the next result.

Proposition 4.12
.

The propagation operator
\mathcal{P}_{k}
defined by (
4.23
) is the unique solution of the problem

\left|\begin{array}[]{l}\textit{Find $P\in\mathscr{L}(H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}))$ such that $\rho(P)<1$ and}\\[10.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{10}_{k}\;P^{2}+(\mathcal{T}^{00}_{k}+\mathcal{T}^{11}_{k})\;P^{\vphantom{0}}+\mathcal{T}^{01}_{k}=0.\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

(4.31)

Proof
.

We only present the outline of the proof which is detailed in
[
23
, Theorem 4.1]
. The existence is directly deduced from (
4.30
). On the other hand, the uniqueness is achieved by considering an operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
which satisfies (
4.31
), and by showing that for any
\Phi\in H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})
, the function defined cell by cell by
\widetilde{U}[\Phi](\cdot+n\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{x})|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}:=E^{0}_{k}[(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})^{n}\,\Phi]+E^{1}_{k}[(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})^{n+1}\,\Phi]
satisfies the same half-guide problem (
\mathscr{P}^{+}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
) as
\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}[\Phi]
, so that both solutions coincide in the half-guide
\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{+}
, and in particular on the interface
\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{1}
:
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\,\Phi=\mathcal{P}_{k}\,\Phi
.

One important point to note from the practical point of view is that the Riccati equation (
4.31
) only involves the local DtN operators
\mathcal{T}^{\ell j}_{k}
which are defined from the solutions of the local cell problems (
4.27
). These problems are computable numerically since they are defined in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
.

Finally, we deduce from (
4.28
) the following expression:

\displaystyle\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.38193pt}\smash{\Lambda}}\vphantom{\Lambda}^{+}_{k}=\mathcal{T}^{10}_{k}\;\mathcal{P}^{\vphantom{0}}_{k}+\mathcal{T}^{00}_{k}.

(4.32)

5
Algorithm and discretization

The method developed in the previous sections can be summarized before discretization as follows:

1
.

For any
{k}\in[-\pi,\pi)
,

(\textit{a})
.

solve the local cell problems (
4.27
) and compute the local DtN operators
\mathcal{T}^{j\ell}_{k}
given by (
4.29
);

(\textit{b})
.

determine the propagation operator
\mathcal{P}_{k}
by solving the Riccati equation (
4.31
);

(\textit{c})
.

deduce the DtN operator
\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.38193pt}\smash{\Lambda}}\vphantom{\Lambda}^{+}_{k}
using (
4.32
);

(\textit{d})
.

adapt Steps

1

1

.(a)
–

1

1

.(c)
for the half-guide
\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{-}
in order to compute the DtN operator
\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.38193pt}\smash{\Lambda}}\vphantom{\Lambda}^{-}_{k}
thanks to cell problems defined in
(-1,0)\times(0,1)^{2}
;

(\textit{e})
.

find the solution
\Phi_{k}
of the interface equation (
4.21
);

(\textit{f})
.

reconstruct the solution
\widehat{U}_{k}[\widehat{G}_{k}]
of the waveguide problem (

FV

#
2

) using (
4.20
) and the cell by cell expression (
4.28
) of
\widehat{U}^{\pm}_{k}[\Phi]
;

2
.

apply the inverse Floquet-Bloch transform (
4.18
) to reconstruct the solution
U[G]
of the augmented strip problem (

P
#

);

3
.

deduce the solution
u
of the
2
D transmission problem (

P

) using (
4.8
), namely
u({\bm{x}})=U({\mathbb{\Theta}}\,{\bm{x}})
.

Since this algorithm operates at a continuous level, it has to be discretized with respect to the space and Floquet variables. In Section
5.1
, we present the semi-discretization procedure with respect to the Floquet variable. In Section
5.2
, we first present the spatial discretization, when one uses the
3
D Finite Element method for the cell problems. However, as this approach is computationally costly, we propose an alternative in Section
5.3
, which turned out to be essential for obtaining our numerical results. More precisely, we show that, due to their anisotropic nature, each local cell problem essentially reduces to a family of
2
D problems. The advantage of this decomposition is that the
2
D problems can be solved independently from one another, and therefore in parallel. As a result, this so-called
Quasi-2D approach
appears to be less costly, though it may seem more intricate at first reading.

5.1
Semi-discretization with respect to the Floquet variable

The solution
U[G]
of the augmented strip problem (

P
#

) is obtained using the inverse Floquet-Bloch transform (
4.18
). In this expression, the integrand involves
\widehat{U}_{k}[\widehat{G}_{k}]
which, in general, can only be computed numerically (modulo an approximation) for a finite number of values of
{k}\in[-\pi,\pi)
(using Step

1

). Thus, the integral with respect to
{k}
in (
4.18
) has to be evaluated using a quadrature rule.

To this end, we consider a regular mesh of
[-\pi,\pi)
consisting of
N_{k}
intervals of equal size
\Delta{k}
, and of
N_{k}+1
equispaced points
({k}_{j})_{0\leq j\leq N_{k}}
, with
N_{k}>0
and
\Delta{k}:=2\pi/N_{k}
. The integral in (
4.18
) is evaluated using a trapezoidal rule using
{k}_{j}
as quadrature points, leading to an approximate solution
U_{\Delta{k}}[G]
of the augmented transmission problem (

FV
#

):

\displaystyle\operatorname{a.e.}\;\textnormal{{x}}=({x},{z}_{1},{z}_{2})\in\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}},\quad\forall\;n\in\mathbb{Z},\quad U_{\Delta{k}}[G](\textnormal{{x}}+n\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})=\frac{\Delta{k}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\sum_{j=0}^{N_{k}}\widehat{U}_{{k}_{j}}[\widehat{G}_{{k}_{j}}]\mskip 1.5mu(\textnormal{{x}})\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{{k}_{j}}({z}_{1}+n)}.

The choice of the trapezoidal rule is motivated in Remark
5.1
.

Remark 5.1
.

If
\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits\omega>0
and
g\in L^{2}(\sigma)
is compactly supported, then similarly to (
1.4
), the solution
U[G]
of (

P
#

) decays exponentially in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{1}
–direction. This implies thanks to Paley-Wiener-type theorems (see e.g.
[
31
, Theorem 2.2.2]
) that the map
{k}\mapsto\widehat{U}_{k}[\widehat{G}_{k}]:={\mathcal{F}}U[G]\,(\cdot,{k})\in H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})
is real analytic.

Moreover, from the properties of the Floquet-Bloch transform, it is clear that
{k}\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu({z}_{1}+n)}\widehat{U}_{k}[\widehat{G}_{k}](\textnormal{{x}})
is
2\pi
–periodic,
\operatorname{a.e.}\;\textnormal{{x}}\in\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
,
\forall\;n\in\mathbb{Z}
. This formal observation justifies the use of the trapezoidal rule, which is known to converge exponentially for smooth periodic integrands (see for instance
[
37
]
).

One way to obtain a precise error estimate is to show that the function
U_{\Delta{k}}[G]
obtained using the trapezoidal rule can be reinterpreted as the solution of a boundary value problem defined in a domain which is bounded in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{1}
–direction. This approach has been studied in detail in
[
7
]
for a different problem, but it can be extended to (

P
#

), thus leading to a quadrature error of the form

\exists\;\alpha>0,\quad\big\|U[G]-U_{\Delta{k}}[G]\big\|_{H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}})}=O(\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\,\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits\omega\,N_{k}}).

5.2
Discretization with respect to the space variable: a first approach

We fix
{k}\in[-\pi,\pi)
, and consider the discretization of Step

1

using the Finite Element method in
3
D. The discretization is a direct adaptation of the procedure described extensively in
[
11
,
15
,
23
]
. For this reason, we do not intend to go into details. We begin with a tetrahedral mesh of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}:=(0,1)^{3}
with step
h>0
. This mesh is assumed to be
periodic
, in the sense that the mesh nodes on the boundary
{x}=0
(
resp.

{z}_{j}=0
) can be identified to those on
{x}=1
(
resp.

{z}_{j}=1
) in a trivial manner. Then, using the classical
H^{1}
–conforming Lagrange Finite Element space of order
d>0
which we call
\mathcal{V}_{h}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})
, an internal approximation of
H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})
and of
H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{j})
is provided by the respective subspaces

\displaystyle\begin{array}[pos]{r@{\ :=\ }l}\mathcal{V}_{h,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})&\big\{V\in\mathcal{V}_{h}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})\ /\ V_{h}|_{{z}_{j}=0}=V_{h}|_{{z}_{j}=1}\ \ \forall\;j\in\{1,2\}\big\},\\[10.00002pt]
\mathcal{V}_{h,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{j})&\big\{V|_{\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{j}}\ /\ V_{h}\in\mathcal{V}_{h}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})\big\},\quad\forall\;j\in\{0,1\}.\end{array}

The periodicity of the mesh allows the identification
\mathcal{V}_{h,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{0})\equiv\mathcal{V}_{h,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{1})\equiv\mathcal{V}_{h,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})
, similarly to the continuous case. In what follows, let
N_{h}:=\dim\mathcal{V}_{h,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})
denote the number of degrees of freedom on the face
\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
.

For any
\Phi_{h}\in\smash{\mathcal{V}_{h,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{j})}
, we solve the Finite Element formulation of the local cell problems (
4.27
), to obtain solutions
E^{j}_{{k},h}[\Phi_{h}]\in\mathcal{V}_{h,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})
,
j\in\{0,1\}
, and we deduce finite-dimensional DtN operators
\smash{\mathcal{T}^{j\ell}_{{k},h}}\in\mathscr{L}(\smash{\mathcal{V}_{h,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{j})})
using the discrete analog of the weak formulation of (
4.29
). Note that these discrete DtN operators can be represented as
N_{h}\times N_{h}
matrices.

To approximate the propagation operator
\mathcal{P}_{k}
, it is natural to introduce the solution
\mathcal{P}_{{k},h}
of the constrained Riccati equation (
4.31
) where
\mathcal{T}^{j\ell}_{k}
is replaced by
\mathcal{T}^{j\ell}_{{k},h}
. As described in
[
23
]
, one can solve the discrete Riccati equation using either
(1)
a
spectral
approach which consists in characterizing
\mathcal{P}_{{k},h}
by means of its eigenpairs which satisfy a quadratic eigenvalue problem, or
(2)
a modified
Newton
method, that is, a standard Newton method with an additional projection step in order to take the spectral radius constraint into account.

From the approximate operator
\mathcal{P}_{{k},h}
, the discrete analog of (
4.32
) allows to define an approximate DtN operator
\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.38193pt}\smash{\Lambda}}\vphantom{\Lambda}^{+}_{{k},h}\in\mathscr{L}(\smash{\mathcal{V}_{h,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{j})})
which can be represented as a
N_{h}\times N_{h}
matrix. We compute
\smash{\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.38193pt}\smash{\Lambda}}\vphantom{\Lambda}^{-}_{{k},h}}
similarly and write the discrete version of the interface equation (
4.21
) as a
N_{h}\times N_{h}
linear system. The corresponding solution
\Phi_{{k},h}\in\smash{\mathcal{V}_{h,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{j})}
is then used to deduce an approximation
\widehat{U}_{{k},h}[\widehat{G}_{k}]
of the waveguide solution of (

FV

#
2

) thanks to (
4.20
) and the cell by cell expression (
4.28
).

Remark 5.2
.

(\textit{a})
.

For
{k}\in[-\pi,\pi)
fixed, an error analysis can be performed for the approximation of the waveguide solution
{\widehat{U}_{k}[\widehat{G}_{k}]}
, assuming that the Riccati equation is solved exactly. To do so, the idea is to show as in
[
11
, Section 2.3.1]
that the discrete solution
\widehat{U}_{{k},h}[\widehat{G}_{k}]
satisfies a discrete half-guide problem on an infinite mesh of
\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
. This observation then allows to use Céa’s lemma and to derive classical Finite Element estimates. In particular, for Lagrange Finite Elements of order
1
, assuming that
\mathbb{A}_{p}
and
\rho_{p}
are smooth enough, one shows that

\big\|\widehat{U}_{k}[\widehat{G}_{k}]-\widehat{U}_{{k},h}[\widehat{G}_{k}]\big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})}=O(h^{2})\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\big\|\widehat{U}_{k}[\widehat{G}_{k}]-\widehat{U}_{{k},h}[\widehat{G}_{k}]\big\|_{H^{1}_{\mathbb{\Theta}}(\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})}=O(h).

(\textit{b})
.

For Configuration (

A

), we recall that
(\mathbb{A}^{+}_{p},\rho^{+}_{p})
are independent of
{z}_{2}
. This property can be used to solve the local cell problems more efficiently. In fact, by expanding
E^{j}_{k}[\Phi]
in a Fourier series with respect to
{z}_{2}
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{a.e.}\;\textnormal{{x}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}},\quad E^{j}_{k}[\Phi](\textnormal{{x}})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{E}^{j}_{{k},n}({x},{z}_{1})\,\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}\pi n{z}_{2}},\quad\widehat{E}^{j}_{{k},n}({x},{z}_{1}):=\int_{0}^{1}E^{j}_{k}[\Phi]({x},{z}_{1},t)\,\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}\pi nt}\,dt,

we note that the
3
D local cell problem satisfied by
E^{j}_{k}[\Phi]
reduces to a countable family (indexed by
n\in\mathbb{Z}
) of decoupled
2
D problems satisfied by the Fourier coefficients
\widehat{E}^{j}_{{k},n}
. The same remark holds for the local cell problems defined on
(-1,0)\times(0,1)^{2}
, since
(\mathbb{A}^{-}_{p},\rho^{-}_{p})
are independent of
{z}_{1}
.

From the discrete solution
\widehat{U}_{{k},h}[\widehat{G}_{k}]
, we compute an approximate solution
U_{\bm{h}}[G]
of the strip problem using Section
5.1
, with
{\bm{h}}:=(h,\Delta{k})
. Finally, for Step

3

, an approximate solution
u_{\bm{h}}
of the
2
–dimensional problem (

P

) is given by
u_{\bm{h}}({\bm{x}}):=\mathrm{E}^{2}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5muU_{\bm{h}}({\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{x}}),\ \forall\;{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, in the spirit of (
4.8
).

The discretization of the local cell problems can be computationally costly, due to the use of
3
D Finite Elements. In the next section, we show that by exploiting the anisotropic nature of the differential operator
-\rho^{-1}_{p}\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\bm{\nabla}}{\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,\bm{\nabla}
, the solution of the local cell problems reduces to a family of independent
2
D cell problems and an interface equation.

5.3
A quasi–2D idea to solve the local cell problems

In this section, we focus on the step

1

1

.(a)
of the solution algorithm, that is, the computation of the local cell solutions
E^{j}_{k}
and the local DtN operators
\mathcal{T}^{j\ell}_{k}
. Although the local cell problems (
4.27
) can be solved directly using
3
–dimensional Finite Elements as in Section
5.2
, it is worth recalling that the differential operator
-\rho^{-1}_{p}\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\bm{\nabla}}{\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,\bm{\nabla}
is strongly linked to the family of operators
-\rho^{-1}_{s}\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\nabla}\mathbb{A}_{s}\!\,\nabla
(
s\in\mathbb{R}
) by means of the chain rule (
1.9
). It is in particular this
“
fibered
”
link that leads to the expression (
4.4
) recalled below:

\displaystyle\operatorname{a.e.}\;({x},{z},s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,1),\quad\widetilde{U}({x},\theta_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{z},\theta_{2}\mskip 1.5mu{z}+s)=u_{s}({x},{z}),

(5.1)

where
U=U[G]
and
u_{s}=u_{s}[g_{s}]
(with
g_{s}(0,{z}):=\widetilde{G}(0,\theta_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{z},\theta_{2}\mskip 1.5mu{z}+s)
) are the respective solutions of (

P
#

) and (

P
s

), and where
\widetilde{U}:=\mathrm{E}^{2}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5muU
and
\widetilde{G}:=\mathrm{E}^{2}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5muG
are respectively the periodic extensions of
U
and
G
in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–direction.

Up to now, in the context of the lifting approach, the relation (
5.1
) has been useful in practice to compute
u_{s}[g_{s}]
(in particular for
s=0
) from
U[G]
. The so-called
quasi
2
–dimensional
(or
quasi-
2
D
) approach developed in this section relies on the converse: in the spirit of (
5.1
), our goal is to reduce the solution of the local cell problems to that of a family of
2
–dimensional decoupled cell problems.

The principle of the quasi-2D method is very similar to the method developed in
[
2
, Section 5.2]
for the
1
D Helmholtz equation with quasiperiodic coefficients. In
[
2
]
, a quasi-1D approach was used to solve a
2
D cell problem with Dirichlet boundary conditions on two opposite edges and periodic conditions on the two other edges (as shown in Figure
16
right). However, the extension to our 3D problems is more delicate, due to the periodicity conditions with respect to
both

{z}_{1}
and
{z}_{2}
. To illustrate this difficulty, it is useful to extend the quasi-1D approach for a
2
D cell problem with periodic conditions on the two pairs of opposite edges. This is the object of the next section.

5.3.1
Illustration of the method in a 2D case

The goal in this section is first to highlight the difficulty of the fibered approach in the case of a particular 2D cell problem with periodic conditions, and then propose a quasi-1D method which will be extended into a quasi-2D method for the 3D cell problems. Throughout this section, the subscript
“
2D
”
is used to emphasize that we are dealing with a 2D case. The generic point is denoted by
{\bm{z}}=({z}_{1},{z}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
\{{\bm{e}}_{1}=(1,0),{\bm{e}}_{2}=(0,1)\}
denotes the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

The
2
D cell problem with periodic conditions

We consider the problem defined in
C:=\{{\bm{z}}=({z}_{1},{z}_{2})\in(0,1)^{2}\}
:
Find
E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l@{\quad}l}\displaystyle\displaystyle-D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,(\mu_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}})-\rho_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,\omega^{2}\,E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}&f_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}&\textnormal{in}\ \ C,\\[12.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}|_{{z}_{j}=0}=E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}|_{{z}_{j}=1}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad(\mu_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}})|_{{z}_{j}=0}=(\mu_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}})|_{{z}_{j}=1}\quad\forall\;j\in\{1,2\},\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

(5.2)

where
\mu_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},\rho_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},f_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
are
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
–periodic, and with
{\bm{\theta}}:=(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
and
D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,:={\bm{\theta}}\cdot\nabla=\theta_{1}\,\partial_{{z}_{1}}+\theta_{2}\,\partial_{{z}_{2}}
. Let
\widetilde{E}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
denote the periodic extension of
E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
in the
{\bm{e}}_{2}
–direction. Using the chain rule and the fact that
E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
is
\mathbb{Z}{\bm{e}}_{2}
–periodic, one shows that the
1
D function
\mathbf{e}_{s}:{z}\mapsto\widetilde{E}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}({z}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{\theta}}+s{\bm{e}}_{2})\in H^{1}(0,1/\theta_{1})
is well-defined for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, and satisfies

\displaystyle-\frac{d}{d{z}}\Big(\mu_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}\,\frac{d\mathbf{e}_{s}}{d{z}}\Big)-\rho_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}\,\omega^{2}\,\mathbf{e}_{s}=f_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}\quad\textnormal{in}\ \ (0,1/\theta_{1}),

(5.3)

with
\mu_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}({z}):=\mu_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}({z}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{\theta}}+s{\bm{e}}_{2})
,
\rho_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}({z}):=\rho_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}({z}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{\theta}}+s{\bm{e}}_{2})
, and
f_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}({z}):=f_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}({z}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{\theta}}+s{\bm{e}}_{2})
. Note also that
\mathbf{e}_{s+1}=\mathbf{e}_{s}
, so that the study of
(\mathbf{e}_{s})_{s}
can be restricted to
s\in[0,1]
.

By inverting the change of variables
(s,{z})\mapsto{z}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{\theta}}+s{\bm{e}}_{2}
, we obtain

\operatorname{a.e.}\;{\bm{z}}=({z}_{1},{z}_{2})\in C,\quad E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}({\bm{z}})=\mathbf{e}_{{z}_{2}-{z}_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vartheta}}({z}_{1}/\theta_{1})\quad\textnormal{with}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vartheta}:=\theta_{2}/\theta_{1}.

(5.4)

The quasi-1D approach would consist in computing
E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
from
\mathbf{e}_{s}
using the above expression. In practice, this expression would be interesting if the
1
D problems satisfied by the functions
\mathbf{e}_{s}
(indexed by
s\in[0,1]
) were decoupled from one another. However, because
E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
is also periodic with respect to
{z}_{1}
, we deduce from (
5.4
) that:

\displaystyle\forall\;s\in\mathbb{R},\quad\mathbf{e}_{s}(0)=\mathbf{e}_{s-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vartheta}}(1/\theta_{1}),

(5.5)

as shown in Figure
16
(left). Equation (
5.5
) makes it impossible to compute the
\mathbf{e}_{s}
independently from one another. The alternative we propose is to relax the coupling (
5.5
) by first replacing the periodicity conditions with respect to
{z}_{1}
by Dirichlet conditions for instance (in order to decouple the solutions of the cell problems), and then by imposing the periodicity afterwards. This is explored in the next paragraph.

per
per
per
per
\bullet
\mathbf{e}_{s-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vartheta}}(1/\theta_{1})
\bullet
\mathbf{e}_{s}(0)
\mathbf{e}_{s-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vartheta}}
\mathbf{e}_{s}
Dirichlet
Dirichlet
per
per
\bullet
\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}(0)=\delta_{j0}
\bullet
\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}(1/\theta_{1})=\delta_{j1}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}}
{z}_{1}
{z}_{2}

Figure 16
:
Left: Since
E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
is periodic with respect to both
{z}_{1}
and
{z}_{2}
, the 1D traces
\mathbf{e}_{s}
and
\mathbf{e}_{s-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vartheta}}
are coupled by sharing a common boundary value (
5.5
). Right: Since
F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
satisfies Dirichlet conditions on
{z}_{1}\in\{0,1\}
, each trace
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}}
satisfies independently a 1D problem with Dirichlet conditions.

Auxiliary local cell problems

To overcome the difficulty induced by the periodic coupling, one can reformulate
E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
in terms of the solutions of
auxiliary
local Dirichlet cell problems for which the quasi-1D method can be applied. For this purpose, we introduce an auxiliary unknown
\varphi\in L^{2}(0,1)
, namely

\varphi:=E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}|_{{z}_{1}=0}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textnormal{(}}=E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}|_{{z}_{1}=1}\ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textnormal{by periodicity of ${z}_{1}\mapsto E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}({z}_{1},{z}_{2})$)}}.

(5.6)

With this choice, by introducing the well-posed cell problems:
Find
F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\equiv F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}[\varphi]
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l@{\quad}l}\displaystyle\displaystyle-D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,(\mu_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}})-\rho_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,\omega^{2}\,F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}&0&\textnormal{in}\ \ C,\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}|_{{z}_{2}=0}=F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}|_{{z}_{2}=1}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad(\mu_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}})|_{{z}_{2}=0}=(\mu_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}})|_{{z}_{2}=1},\hfil\lx@intercol\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}|_{{z}_{1}=0}=\varphi\quad\textnormal{and}\quad F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}|_{{z}_{1}=1}=\varphi\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

and:
Find
\mathcal{G}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}\equiv\mathcal{G}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}[f_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}]
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l@{\quad}l}\displaystyle\displaystyle-D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,(\mu_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,\mathcal{G}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}})-\rho_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,\omega^{2}\,\mathcal{G}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}&f_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}&\textnormal{in}\ \ C,\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\mathcal{G}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}|_{{z}_{2}=0}=\mathcal{G}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}|_{{z}_{2}=1}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad(\mu_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,\mathcal{G}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}})|_{{z}_{2}=0}=(\mu_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,\mathcal{G}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}})|_{{z}_{2}=1},\hfil\lx@intercol\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\mathcal{G}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}|_{{z}_{1}=0}=0\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mathcal{G}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}|_{{z}_{1}=1}=0,\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

we obtain by linearity that

E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}=F_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}[\varphi]+\mathcal{G}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}[f_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}].

(5.7)

The important difference between the problems satisfied by
F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
,
\mathcal{G}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}
, and the one satisfied by
E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
, is that the periodicity conditions on
\{{z}_{1}=0\}
and
\{{z}_{1}=1\}
have been replaced by Dirichlet boundary conditions. Hence,
F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}
can be solved using the quasi-1D approach described in
[
2
]
, as we will describe later, in Proposition
5.3
.

The auxiliary unknown
\varphi

We now explain how to characterize the unknown
\varphi
. By imposing the periodicity of
\mu_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
with respect to
{z}_{1}
in (
5.7
), we deduce that

\displaystyle(\Xi^{0}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}+\Xi^{1}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}})\,\varphi=-(\Upsilon^{0}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}+\Upsilon^{1}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}})\,f_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},

(5.8)

where the auxiliary local DtN operators
\Xi^{j}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
and the right-hand sides
\Upsilon^{j}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}\,f
,
j\in\{0,1\}
are given by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ :=\ }l}\Xi^{j}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,\varphi&(-1)^{j+1}\,(\mu_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,F_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}[\varphi])|_{{z}_{1}=j},\\[12.0pt]
\Upsilon^{j}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}\,f&(-1)^{j+1}\,(\mu_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}\,D^{\vphantom{+}}_{\bm{\theta}}\,\mathcal{G}_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}}[f])|_{{z}_{1}=j}.\end{array}\right.

Conversely, it can be shown using the presence of absorption that
\Xi^{0}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}+\Xi^{1}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
is invertible, so that (
5.8
) admits a unique solution.

The fibered structure of the auxiliary local cell problems

In order to emphasize the fibered structure of
F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},\mathcal{G}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
, consider the well-posed
1
D problems given for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
j\in\{0,1\}
by:
Find
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}}\in H^{1}(0,1/\theta_{1})
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l}\displaystyle-\frac{d}{d{z}}\Big(\mu_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}\,\frac{d{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}}}{d{z}}\Big)-\rho_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}\,\omega^{2}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}}&0\quad\textnormal{in}\ \ (0,1/\theta_{1}),\\[4.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{0}_{s}(0)=1,\quad\mathbf{f}^{0}_{s}(1/\theta_{1})}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0},\\[4.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{1}_{s}(0)=0,\quad\mathbf{f}^{1}_{s}(1/\theta_{1})}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1},\end{array}\right.

and:
Find
\mathbf{g}_{s}=\mathbf{g}_{s}[f_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}]\in H^{1}(0,1/\theta_{1})
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l}\displaystyle-\frac{d}{d{z}}\Big(\mu_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}\,\frac{d\mathbf{g}_{s}}{d{z}}\Big)-\rho_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}\,\omega^{2}\,\mathbf{g}_{s}&f_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}\quad\textnormal{in}\ \ (0,1/\theta_{1}),\\[4.0pt]
\mathbf{g}_{s}(0)=\mathbf{g}_{s}(1/\theta_{1})&0.\end{array}\right.

Note that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s+1}=\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}}
and
\mathbf{g}_{s+1}=\mathbf{g}_{s}
. The advantage of resorting to the auxiliary solutions
F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},\mathcal{G}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
lies in the following result, which highlight their fibered or so-called
quasi-1D
structure.

Proposition 5.3

(
[
2
, Proposition 5.1]
)
.

For any
(\varphi,f_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}})\in L^{2}(0,1)\times L^{2}(C)
, we have

\operatorname{a.e.}\;({z},s)\in(0,1/\theta_{1})\times(0,1),\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l}\widetilde{F}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}(\theta_{1}{z},\theta_{2}{z}+s)&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varphi(s)\,\mathbf{f}^{0}_{s}({z})+\varphi(s+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vartheta})\,\mathbf{f}^{1}_{s}({z})},\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vartheta}:=\theta_{2}/\theta_{1},\\[12.0pt]
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}(\theta_{1}{z},\theta_{2}{z}+s)&\mathbf{g}_{s}({z}),\end{array}\right.

(5.9)

with
\mathbf{g}_{s}=\mathbf{g}_{s}[f_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}]
, and where
\widetilde{F}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
(
resp.

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
) denotes the periodic extension of
F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
(
resp.

\mathcal{G}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
) in the
{\bm{e}}_{2}
–direction, with
F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}=F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}[\varphi]
and
\mathcal{G}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}=\mathcal{G}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}[f_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}]
.

Proposition
5.3
allows to compute
F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}[\varphi]
(
resp.

\mathcal{G}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}[f_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}]
) from the
1
D functions
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}}
(
resp.

\mathbf{g}_{s}[f_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}]
),
s\in[0,1]
. The difference with
\mathbf{e}_{s}
is that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}}
and
\mathbf{g}_{s}[f_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}]
satisfy
1
D problems which can be solved independently from one another in practice, and hence in parallel (see Figure
16
right). Note also that the operators
\Xi^{j}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},\Upsilon^{j}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
can be computed using
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}}
and
\mathbf{g}_{s}[f_{{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},s}]
. We refer to
[
2
, Proposition 5.2]
, where a similar situation is presented.

Algorithm for the quasi-1D method

The approach presented above to compute the solution
E_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
of (
5.2
) can be summarized as follows:

•

Compute the solutions
F_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},\mathcal{G}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
of the auxiliary cell problems using the solutions
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}},\mathbf{g}_{s}
of the
1
D problems indexed by
s\in[0,1]
, and which can be solved in parallel;

•

Construct
\Xi^{j}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}},\Upsilon^{j}_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
, and deduce the auxiliary unknown
\varphi
by solving (
5.8
).

The discretization of the first item is described in
[
2
, Section 5.2.2]
. The difference with the quasi-1D method in
[
2
]
is the second item above. In practice, (
5.8
) is a dense system of size
N\times N
, where
N
is the number of degrees of freedom on the edge
{z}_{1}=0
. Therefore, instead of solving a
2
D problem of size
N^{2}
, one can solve
N
independent
1
D problems of size
N
in parallel, and invert a
N
–sized dense matrix, which encodes the coupling between the
1
D solutions.

5.3.2
Extension to the 3D local cell problems

In what follows, the Floquet variable
{k}\in(-\pi,\pi)
is fixed, and is omitted in some of the notations. We now extend the idea developed in the previous section to the case of the 3D local cell problems (
4.27
) which are recalled under the formal form:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l}\displaystyle-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})}\,{\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,E^{j}_{k}-\rho_{p}\,\omega^{2}\,E^{j}_{k}&0\quad\textnormal{in}\ \ \mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}},\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\textnormal{$E^{j}_{k}$ is $1$--periodic with respect to ${z}_{1}$ and ${z}_{2}$,}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil E^{0}_{k}[\Phi]|_{\Sigma^{0}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}=\Phi\quad\textnormal{and}\quad E^{0}_{k}[\Phi]|_{\Sigma^{1}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}=0,\hfil\lx@intercol\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil E^{1}_{k}[\Phi]|_{\Sigma^{0}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}=0\quad\textnormal{and}\quad E^{1}_{k}[\Phi]|_{\Sigma^{1}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}=\Phi,\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

(5.10)

where
\Phi
is a fixed surface data, which plays a similar role than the volume source term
f_{{{\textnormal{{2D}}}}}
in the previous section (see (
5.3
)). By analogy with the previous section, we want to take advantage of the quasi-2D nature of these 3D problems, to deduce
E^{j}_{k}
from the solution of
2
D cell problems set in
({\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\mathbb{R}^{2}+s\,\textnormal{{e}}_{2})\cap\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}
and parameterized by
s\in[0,1]
. However, due to the periodicity conditions with respect to
{z}_{1}
and
{z}_{2}
, the traces of
E^{j}_{k}
on the hyperplane
{\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\mathbb{R}^{2}+s\,\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
,
s\in[0,1]
, satisfy a coupling relation that prevents them from being computed independently from one another. For this reason, we introduce auxiliary Dirichlet cell problems that have the desired fibered structure. The link between
E^{j}_{k}
and the solutions of these auxiliary problems is related to an auxiliary unknown, chosen as the trace of
E^{j}_{k}
on the interface
\{{z}_{1}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}\}
, and which satisfies an equation similar to (
5.8
).

The auxiliary local cell problems

Consider
E^{0}_{k}
for simplicity. We introduce a Dirichlet-to-Dirichlet operator defined by

\displaystyle\forall\;\Phi\in H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}),\quad\mathcal{R}^{0}\Phi:=E^{0}_{k}[\Phi]|_{{z}_{1}=0}\ \ (=E^{0}_{k}[\Phi]|_{{z}_{1}=1}\ \ \textnormal{by periodicity of ${z}_{1}\mapsto E^{0}_{k}[\Phi](\textnormal{{x}})$}).

(5.11)

Then for any
\Phi,\Psi
, using the respective solutions
F[\Psi]
and
\mathcal{G}^{0}[\Phi]
of the well-posed Dirichlet cell problems (see Figure
17
):

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l@{\quad}l}\displaystyle\displaystyle-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})}\,{\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,F-\rho_{p}\,\omega^{2}\,F&0&\textnormal{in}\ \ \mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}},\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\textnormal{$F=F[\Psi]$ is $1$--periodic with respect to ${z}_{2}$,}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil F[\Psi]|_{{x}=0}=0\quad\textnormal{and}\quad F[\Psi]|_{{x}=1}=0,\hfil\lx@intercol\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil F[\Psi]|_{{z}_{1}=0}=\Psi\quad\textnormal{and}\quad F[\Psi]|_{{z}_{1}=1}=\Psi,\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

(5.12)

and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l@{\quad}l}\displaystyle\displaystyle-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})}\,{\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,\mathcal{G}^{0}-\rho_{p}\,\omega^{2}\,\mathcal{G}^{0}&0&\textnormal{in}\ \ \mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}},\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\textnormal{$\mathcal{G}^{0}=\mathcal{G}^{0}[\Phi]$ is $1$--periodic with respect to ${z}_{2}$,}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\mathcal{G}^{0}[\Phi]|_{{x}=0}=\Phi\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mathcal{G}^{0}[\Phi]|_{{x}=1}=0,\hfil\lx@intercol\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\mathcal{G}^{0}[\Phi]|_{{z}_{1}=0}=0\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mathcal{G}^{0}[\Phi]|_{{z}_{1}=1}=0,\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

(5.13)

it follows by linearity that

\displaystyle E^{0}_{k}[\Phi]=F[\mathcal{R}^{0}\Phi]+\mathcal{G}^{0}[\Phi].

(5.14)

The main interest of the problems (
5.12
,
5.13
) satisfied by
F[\Psi]
and
\mathcal{G}^{0}[\Phi]
is that although they remain periodic in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{2}
–direction, they involve Dirichlet conditions on the faces
\{{x}=\ell\}
and
\{{z}_{1}=\ell\}
. For this reason, these problems can be interpreted as a concatenation of
2
D cell problems, as we shall describe later, in Proposition
5.5
.

.

For a fixed
\Phi
,
\mathcal{R}^{0}\Phi
plays the same role as the auxiliary unknown
\varphi
defined by (
5.6
).

\Phi
per
0
per
E^{0}_{k}[\Phi]
0
\Psi
0
\Psi
F[\Psi]
\Phi
0

\mathcal{G}^{0}[\Phi]

:
Dirichlet conditions satisfied by
E^{0}_{k}[\Phi]
,

, and
