.

Characterization of the Dirichlet-to-Dirichlet operator
\mathcal{R}^{0}

Before highlighting the advantage of introducing
F
and
\mathcal{G}^{0}
, let us derive an equation to characterize
\mathcal{R}^{0}
. The periodicity of
E^{0}_{k}
in
{z}_{1}
leads to the following equality

\displaystyle\big({\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,E^{0}_{k}[\Phi]\big)\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{1}|_{{z}_{1}=0}=\big({\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,E^{0}_{k}[\Phi]\big)\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{1}|_{{z}_{1}=1},

which reformulates as

\forall\;\Phi\in H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}),\quad(\Xi^{0}+\Xi^{1})\,\mathcal{R}^{0}\Phi=-(\Upsilon^{00}+\Upsilon^{01})\,\Phi,

(5.15)

with
\Xi^{\ell}
and
\Upsilon^{0\ell}
,
\ell\in\{0,1\}
, being the local auxiliary DtN operators defined by

\displaystyle\forall\;\Phi,\Psi,\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ :=\ }l}\Xi^{\ell}\,\Psi&(-1)^{\ell+1}\,\big({\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,F[\Psi]\big)\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{1}|_{{z}_{1}=\ell},\\[12.0pt]
\Upsilon^{0\ell}\,\Phi&(-1)^{\ell+1}\,\big({\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,\mathcal{G}^{0}[\Phi]\big)\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{1}|_{{z}_{1}=\ell}.\end{array}\right.

(5.16)

Conversely, it can be shown using the presence of absorption that
\Xi^{0}+\Xi^{1}
is invertible, so that (
5.15
) is well-posed.

The above arguments extend naturally to
E^{1}_{k}
, to which we can associate
\mathcal{R}^{1}
,
\mathcal{G}^{1}
, and
\Upsilon^{1\ell}
by adapting respectively (
5.11
), (
5.13
), and (
5.16
). Then one has

\forall\;\Phi\in H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}),\quad E^{1}_{k}[\Phi]=F[\mathcal{R}^{1}\Phi]+\mathcal{G}^{1}[\Phi],\quad\textnormal{where}\quad(\Xi^{0}+\Xi^{1})\,\mathcal{R}^{1}=-(\Upsilon^{10}+\Upsilon^{11}).

(5.17)

From
\mathcal{R}^{0}
and
\mathcal{R}^{1}
, one can also deduce the local DtN operators
\mathcal{T}^{j\ell}_{k}
defined by (
4.29
). More precisely, using (
5.14
,
5.15
) and (
5.17
), it follows by linearity that

\forall\;j,\ell\in\{0,1\},\quad\mathcal{T}^{j\ell}_{k}=\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{j\ell}+\widetilde{\Xi}^{\ell}\,\mathcal{R}^{j},

(5.18)

where
\widetilde{\Xi}^{\ell}
and
\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{j\ell}
are given by

\displaystyle\forall\;\Phi,\Psi,\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ :=\ }l}\widetilde{\Xi}^{\ell}\,\Psi&(-1)^{\ell+1}\,\big({\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,F[\Psi]\big)\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{x}|_{{x}=\ell},\\[12.0pt]
\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{j\ell}\,\Phi&(-1)^{\ell+1}\,\big({\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,\mathcal{G}^{j}[\Phi]\big)\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{1}|_{{x}=\ell}.\end{array}\right.

(5.19)

It is worth noting that the operators
\Xi^{\ell}
,
\widetilde{\Xi}^{\ell}
,
\Upsilon^{j\ell}
,
\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{j\ell}
can all be obtained by computing
F
and
\mathcal{G}^{j}
, which satisfy cell problems with Dirichlet conditions on
both
the boundaries
\{{x}=\ell\}
and
\{{z}_{1}=\ell\}
. We now highlight the structure of
F
and
\mathcal{G}^{j}
.

The fibered structure of the auxiliary cell problems

Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
, using the definition (
4.3
) of
(\mathbb{A}_{s},\rho_{s})
, let us introduce the 2D cell problems defined in the cell
Q:=\{{\bm{x}}=({x},{z})\in(0,1)\times(0,1/\theta_{1})\}
and for any
j\in\{0,1\}
as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l@{\quad}l}\displaystyle\displaystyle-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut(\nabla+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu\theta_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{e}}_{z})}\,\mathbb{A}_{s}\,(\nabla+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu\theta_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{e}}_{z})\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}}-\rho_{s}\,\omega^{2}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}}&0&\textnormal{in}\ \ Q,\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}[\psi]}|_{{x}=0}=0\quad\textnormal{and}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}[\psi]}|_{{x}=1}=0,\hfil\lx@intercol\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}[\psi]|_{{z}=\ell}=\delta_{j\ell}\,\psi,}\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

(5.20)

and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l@{\quad}l}\displaystyle\displaystyle-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut(\nabla+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu\theta_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{e}}_{z})}\,\mathbb{A}_{s}\,(\nabla+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu\theta_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{e}}_{z})\,\mathbf{g}^{j}_{s}-\rho_{s}\,\omega^{2}\,\mathbf{g}^{j}_{s}&0&\textnormal{in}\ \ Q,\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\mathbf{g}^{j}_{s}[\varphi]|_{{x}=\ell}=\delta_{j\ell}\,\varphi,\hfil\lx@intercol\\[8.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\mathbf{g}^{j}_{s}[\varphi]|_{{z}=0}=0\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mathbf{g}^{j}_{s}[\varphi]|_{{z}=1/\theta_{1}}=0.\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

(5.21)

We also introduce for
\ell\in\{0,1\}
,
\Gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{\ell}:=(0,1)\times\{\ell\}\times(0,1)
and the edges (see Figure
18
)

\widehat{I}^{\mathtt{X}}_{\ell}:=\{\ell\}\times(0,1/\theta_{1}),\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\widehat{I}^{\mathtt{Z}}_{\ell}:=(0,1)\times\{\ell/\theta_{1}\}.

Then, the link between the solution
F
(
resp

\mathcal{G}^{j}
) of (
5.12
) (
resp
(
5.13
) for
j=0
) and
\mathbf{f}_{s}
(
resp

\mathbf{g}^{j}_{s}
) is given by the next result, which mirrors Proposition
5.3
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\Psi\in L^{2}(\Gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{j})
, for
j\in\{0,1\}
, be such that
\psi_{s}:={\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}\Psi(\cdot,s):{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{x}\mapsto\mathrm{E}^{2}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5mu\Psi({x},0,s)}
belongs to
H^{1/2}(\widehat{I}^{\mathtt{Z}}_{j})
for almost any
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Recall that
\vartheta:=\theta_{2}/\theta_{1}
. Then one has

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\operatorname{a.e.}\;({x},{z},s)\in Q\times(0,1),\quad\mathrm{E}^{2}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5muF({x},\theta_{1}\,{z},\theta_{2}\,{z}+s)=\mathbf{f}^{0}_{s}[\psi_{s}]({x},{z})+\mathbf{f}^{1}_{s}[\psi_{s+\vartheta}]({x},{z})},

(5.22)

where
F=F[\Psi]
and
(\mathbf{f}^{0}_{s},\mathbf{f}^{1}_{s})
denote the respective solutions of (
5.12
) and (
5.20
). Similarly, for any
\Phi\in H^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{j})
, by setting
\varphi_{s}:={\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}\Phi(\cdot,s){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:{z}\mapsto\mathrm{E}^{2}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5mu\Phi(0,\theta_{1}\,{z},\theta_{2}\,{z}+s)}
for almost any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, one has

\displaystyle\operatorname{a.e.}\;({x},{z},s)\in Q\times(0,1),\quad\mathrm{E}^{2}_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathcal{G}^{j}({x},\theta_{1}\,{z},\theta_{2}\,{z}+s)=\mathbf{g}^{j}_{s}({x},{z}),

(5.23)

where
\mathcal{G}^{j}=\mathcal{G}^{j}[\Phi]
and
\mathbf{g}^{j}_{s}=\mathbf{g}^{j}_{s}[\varphi_{s}]
are the respective solutions of (
5.13
) and (
5.21
).

\Gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{1}

with its faces

Proposition
5.5
shows that computing
(F,\mathcal{G}^{j})
reduces to finding
(\mathbf{f}_{s},\mathbf{g}^{j}_{s})
for any
s\in(0,1)
. The advantage in solving the problems satisfied by
(\mathbf{f}_{s},\mathbf{g}^{j}_{s})
is that they are 2-dimensional, and can be solved independently from one another (with respect to
s
), and therefore in parallel.

Finally, let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{t}^{j\ell}_{s}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{\mathbf{t}}^{j\ell}_{s}}
, and
\bm{\upsilon}^{j\ell}
,
\widetilde{\bm{\upsilon}}^{j\ell}
,
j,\ell\in\{0,1\}
be the auxiliary edge DtN operators defined by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ :=\ }l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{t}^{j\ell}_{s}\psi}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(-1)^{\ell+1}\big(\mathbb{A}_{s}\,(\nabla+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu\theta_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{e}}_{z})\,\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}[\psi]\big)\cdot{\bm{e}}_{z}|_{\widehat{I}^{\mathtt{Z}}_{\ell}}},\\[12.0pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{\mathbf{t}}^{j\ell}_{s}\psi}&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(-1)^{\ell+1}\big(\mathbb{A}_{s}\,(\nabla+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu\theta_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{e}}_{z})\,\mathbf{f}^{j}_{s}[\psi]\big)\cdot{\bm{e}}_{x}|_{\widehat{I}^{\mathtt{X}}_{\ell}}},\\[12.0pt]
\bm{\upsilon}^{j\ell}_{s}\varphi&(-1)^{\ell+1}\big(\mathbb{A}_{s}\,(\nabla+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu\theta_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{e}}_{z})\,\mathbf{g}^{j}_{s}[\varphi]\big)\cdot{\bm{e}}_{z}|_{\widehat{I}^{\mathtt{Z}}_{\ell}},\\[12.0pt]
\widetilde{\bm{\upsilon}}^{j\ell}_{s}\varphi&(-1)^{\ell+1}\big(\mathbb{A}_{s}\,(\nabla+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu\theta_{1}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{e}}_{z})\,\mathbf{g}^{j}_{s}[\varphi]\big)\cdot{\bm{e}}_{z}|_{\widehat{I}^{\mathtt{X}}_{\ell}}.\end{array}\right.

(5.24)

Then the DtN operators
\Xi^{\ell}
,
\widetilde{\Xi}^{\ell}
,
\Upsilon^{j\ell}
,
\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{j\ell}
can be derived from the edge DtN operators
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{t}^{j\ell}_{s}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{\mathbf{t}}^{j\ell}_{s}}
, and
\bm{\upsilon}^{j\ell}
,
\widetilde{\bm{\upsilon}}^{j\ell}
, as highlighted in the next result, which follows directly from the weak forms of these operators and from the duality property (
3.25
) which can also be extended to the faces
\Gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{\ell}
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Recall that
\vartheta:=\theta_{2}/\theta_{1}
. For
\ell\in\{0,1\}
, let
(\Phi^{\ell},\Psi^{\ell})\in L^{2}(\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{\ell})\times L^{2}(\Gamma^{\ell}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}})
be such that for almost any
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
\varphi^{\ell}_{s}:={\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}\Phi^{\ell}(\cdot,s)\in H^{1/2}(\widehat{I}^{\mathtt{X}}_{\ell})
and
\psi^{\ell}_{s}:={\mathscr{S}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\mskip 1.5mu}\Psi(\cdot,s)\in H^{1/2}(\widehat{I}^{\mathtt{Z}}_{\ell})
. Then

\forall\;j,\ell\in\{0,1\},\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l}\big\langle\Xi^{\ell}\,\Psi^{j},\;\Psi^{\ell}\big\rangle_{\Gamma^{\ell}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}&\displaystyle\frac{1}{\theta_{1}}\int_{0}^{1}\big\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{t}^{0\ell}_{s}\,\psi^{j}_{s}+\mathbf{t}^{1\ell}_{s}\,\psi^{j}_{s+\vartheta}},\;\psi^{\ell}_{s}\big\rangle_{\widehat{I}^{\mathtt{Z}}_{\ell}}\;ds,\\[12.0pt]
\big\langle\widetilde{\Xi}^{\ell}\,\Psi^{j},\;\Phi^{\ell}\big\rangle_{\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{\ell}}&\displaystyle\frac{1}{\theta_{1}}\int_{0}^{1}\big\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{\mathbf{t}}^{0\ell}_{s}\,\psi^{j}_{s}+\widetilde{\mathbf{t}}^{1\ell}_{s}\,\psi^{j}_{s+\vartheta}},\;\varphi^{\ell}_{s}\big\rangle_{\widehat{I}^{\mathtt{X}}_{\ell}}\;ds,\\[12.0pt]
\big\langle\Upsilon^{j\ell}\,\Phi^{j},\;\Psi^{\ell}\big\rangle_{\Gamma^{\ell}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}&\displaystyle\frac{1}{\theta_{1}}\int_{0}^{1}\big\langle\bm{\upsilon}^{j\ell}_{s}\,\varphi^{j}_{s},\;\psi^{\ell}_{s}\big\rangle_{\widehat{I}^{\mathtt{Z}}_{\ell}}\;ds,\\[12.0pt]
\big\langle\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{j\ell}\,\Phi^{j},\;\Phi^{\ell}\big\rangle_{\Sigma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{\ell}}&\displaystyle\frac{1}{\theta_{1}}\int_{0}^{1}\big\langle\widetilde{\bm{\upsilon}}^{j\ell}_{s}\,\varphi^{j}_{s},\;\varphi^{\ell}_{s}\big\rangle_{\widehat{I}^{\mathtt{X}}_{\ell}}\;ds.\end{array}\right.

(5.25)

The algorithm for the quasi-2D method

Let us summarize the quasi-2D method in the following algorithm.

•

solve the 2D local cell problems (
5.20
,
5.21
) and compute the auxiliary edge DtN operators
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{t}^{j\ell}_{s}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{\mathbf{t}}^{j\ell}_{s}}
, and
\bm{\upsilon}^{j\ell}
,
\widetilde{\bm{\upsilon}}^{j\ell}
given by (
5.24
) for any
s\in[0,1]
;

•

Compute the auxiliary DtN operators
\Xi^{j}
,
\widetilde{\Xi}^{\ell}
,
\Upsilon^{j\ell}
,
\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{j\ell}
using (
5.25
);

•

Determine the DtD operator
\mathcal{R}^{j}
,
j\in\{0,1\}
, by solving the linear equation (
5.15
);

•

Deduce
E^{j}_{k}
, and compute the local DtN operators
\mathcal{T}^{j\ell}_{k}
using their expression (
5.18
) with respect to
\mathcal{R}^{j}
and
\Xi^{j}
,
\widetilde{\Xi}^{\ell}
,
\Upsilon^{j\ell}
,
\widetilde{\Upsilon}^{j\ell}
.

We do not describe the discretization of this algorithm, since it is an extension of the quasi-1D method. We note that in practice, the discrete counterpart of the equation (
5.15
) used to compute
\mathcal{R}^{j}
is a dense system of size
N^{2}\times N^{2}
, where
N
is the number of degrees of freedom on the edge
\{{x}\in[0,1],\ {z}_{1}={z}_{2}=0\}
. In other words, instead of solving
3
D problems of size
N^{3}
, the quasi-2D method allows to solve
N
independent
2
D cell problems of size
N^{2}
in parallel, and invert a
N^{2}
–sized dense matrix which encodes the coupling between the
2
D solutions. Once the DtN operators
\mathcal{T}^{j\ell}_{k}
have been approximated, they can be used to derive the propagation operator
\mathcal{P}_{k}
(using the Riccati equation (
4.31
)), and the DtN operator
\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.38193pt}\smash{\Lambda}}\vphantom{\Lambda}^{+}_{k}
(using (
4.32
)). Moreover, the local cell solutions
E^{j}_{k}
can be used to reconstruct the half-guide solution
\widehat{U}^{+}_{k}
cell by cell.

6
Numerical results

This section provides a series of numerical results with the goal to validate the method in various situations. For the sole sake of simplicity, simulations are performed with the tensor
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{I}_{2}
. Unless otherwise specified, the jump data
g
is a cut-off function, and the augmented data
G
is constant with respect to
{z}_{2}
:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\quad}r@{\ :=\ }l}\forall\;{z}\in\mathbb{R},&g(0,{z})&100\,\phi(2{z}),\quad\textnormal{with}\quad\phi({z}):=\exp\big(1-1/(1-{z}^{2})\big)\,\mathds{1}_{[-1,1]}({z}),\\[12.0pt]
\forall\;{z}_{1}\in\mathbb{R},&G(0,{z}_{1},{z}_{2})&g(0,{z}_{1}/\theta_{1}).\end{array}\right.

(6.1)

Simulations are carried out using Lagrange finite elements of order
1
on a regular triangular mesh, and we use the quasi-2D method to solve the local cell problems.

6.1
Validation in the homogeneous setting

In the first example, we consider the case where
\rho
is piecewise constant:

\forall\;{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\rho^{+}({\bm{x}}):=1\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\rho^{-}({\bm{x}}):=2.

This coefficient falls within the scope of both Configurations (

A

) and (

B

) with any periodicity parameter. For Configuration (

A

), we choose
p^{+}_{z}:=1
and
p^{-}_{z}:=\sqrt{2}
to be the periods on both sides of the interface. For Configuration (

B

), we choose
{\bm{p}}^{+}:=(\sqrt{2},1)
as the periodicity vector in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
. The jump data
g
and its extension
G
are given by (
6.1
).

The reference solution
u_{\textit{ref}}
used in this context is obtained by applying the partial Fourier transform with respect to
{z}
, and by solving analytically a family of transmission problems defined on
\mathbb{R}
and parameterized by the Fourier dual variable. In fact, it can be computed that

\displaystyle\operatorname{a.e.}\;({x},{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\pm},\quad u_{\textit{ref}}\mskip 1.5mu({x},{z})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\widehat{g}_{\zeta}}{r^{+}_{\zeta}+r^{-}_{\zeta}}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mp r^{\pm}_{\zeta}\mskip 1.5mu{x}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu\zeta\mskip 1.5mu{z}}\;d\zeta,\quad\textnormal{with}\quad\widehat{g}_{\zeta}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}g({z})\;\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu\zeta\mskip 1.5mu{z}}\;d{z},

and where
r^{\pm}_{\zeta}
are defined for any
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
by
(r^{\pm}_{\zeta})^{2}=\zeta^{2}-\rho^{\pm}\,\omega^{2}
,
\mathop{\mathfrak{Re}}\nolimits r^{\pm}_{\zeta}\geq 0
.

The solutions that follow from applying the lifting approach to Configurations (

A

) and (

B

) are compared in Figure
19
to
u_{\textit{ref}}
. We first choose
\omega=8+0.25\,\mathrm{i}
and a mesh step
h=0.025
, which corresponds approximately to
31
points per wavelength in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
and
22
points per wavelength in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-}
. The number of Floquet points is set to
N_{k}=64
. The visual similarity between the results validates qualitatively the method in the homogeneous setting.

\rho
. Using the cut-off function
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
defined in (

), we start from the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
–periodic functions defined in one periodicity cell by

\displaystyle\forall\;\accentset{\circ}{{\bm{x}}}=(\accentset{\circ}{{x}},\accentset{\circ}{{z}})\in(0,1)^{2},\quad\accentset{\circ}{\rho}^{-}(\accentset{\circ}{{\bm{x}}}):=0.5+\phi(4\accentset{\circ}{{x}})\,\phi(4\accentset{\circ}{{z}})\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\accentset{\circ}{\rho}^{+}(\accentset{\circ}{{\bm{x}}}):=0.5+\phi(2.5|\accentset{\circ}{{\bm{x}}}|).

), we use

\forall\;{\bm{x}}=({x},{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\rho^{\pm}({\bm{x}}):=\accentset{\circ}{\rho}^{\pm}({x},{z}/p^{\pm}_{z}),

(6.3)

which is
p^{\pm}_{z}
–periodic with respect to

. For Configuration (

B

), we set

\displaystyle\forall\;{\bm{x}}=({x},{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\rho^{-}({\bm{x}})=1\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\rho^{+}({\bm{x}}):=\accentset{\circ}{\rho}({x}-(p^{+}_{x}/p^{+}_{z})\mskip 1.5mu{z},{z}/p^{+}_{z}),

(6.4)

so that
\rho^{+}
is
\mathbb{Z}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{e}}_{x}+\mathbb{Z}\,{\bm{p}}^{+}
–periodic with
{\bm{p}}^{+}=(p^{+}_{x},p^{+}_{z})
. These coefficients are shown in Figure

.

6.2

We consider a coefficient
\rho
which is
1
–periodic in the direction of the interface (see Figures

and

left). In this case, as done in
[

]
, one can directly apply a Floquet-Bloch transform in the direction of the interface, leading to a family of 2D transmission problems defined in
\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)
and parameterized by the Floquet dual variable. Each of the waveguide problems can then be reduced to an interface equation featuring 2D DtN operators, which we obtain by computing the solution of half-guide problems defined in
\mathbb{R}_{\pm}\times(0,1)
. Solving these half-guide problems involves 2D local cell problems and a propagation operator, similarly to Section
4.4
. We use this approach to construct a reference solution
u_{\textit{ref}}
, to which we compare the solution
u
obtained using the lifting approach. We fix
\omega=8+0.25\mskip 1.5mu\mathrm{i}

\rho

), with

. Figure

shows the solution
u_{\bm{h}}
computed numerically using the lifting approach for a mesh step
h=0.025
and
N_{k}=64
. As expected, this solution is close to the reference solution
u_{\textit{ref}}
.

Invariance with respect to the period

We only consider Configuration (

A

) for simplicity. As the coefficient
\rho^{\pm}
is
p^{\pm}_{z}
–periodic with respect to
{z}
, it is also

{z}
for any
\ell^{\pm}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
. Choosing
\ell^{\pm}\,p^{\pm}_{z}
to be the period along the interface will leave the
2

u
of (

P

) unchanged since
(\mathbb{A},\rho)
remain the same, whereas the
3

U[G]
of (

P
#

) will be modified, due to the different expressions of the augmented coefficients
(\mathbb{A}_{p},\rho_{p})
, the cut matrix
{\mathbb{\Theta}}
, and the augmented jump data
G
. To see if the approximate solution
u_{\bm{h}}
has the same invariances as

with respect to the periods, we compute
u_{\bm{h}}
and the

in Figure

for

,

, and

. For both values of the periods, the trace of

on the interface

is shown in Figure

. One sees that
U_{\bm{h}}
changes with respect to
(p^{+}_{z},p^{-}_{z})
, whereas
u_{\bm{h}}
remains approximately the same.

Invariance with respect to augmented jump data

We still consider Configuration (

A

) for simplicity. Now, let us look at the invariance of the solution
u_{\bm{h}}
with respect to the augmented jump data
G
. Since the data
g
used for our experiments is smooth, we recall that the assumptions on the augmented jump data reduce to
G
satisfying
G(0,\theta_{1}\,{z},\theta_{2}\,{z})=g(0,{z})
and
G(\cdot+\textnormal{{e}}_{2})=G
. These requirements are satisfied by the following functions (see Remark
4.5
):

\displaystyle G_{1}(0,{z}_{1},{z}_{2}):=g(0,{z}_{1}/\theta_{1})\quad\textnormal{and}\quad G_{2}(0,{z}_{1},{z}_{2}):=\exp\big(2\mathrm{i}\pi\mskip 1.5mu({z}_{2}-{z}_{1}\theta_{2}/\theta_{1})\big)\;g(0,{z}_{1}/\theta_{1}).

Figure
26
(middle and right columns) and Figure
27
show the solution
u_{\bm{h}}
and the augmented solution
U_{\bm{h}}
obtained for
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, with
p^{+}_{z}=1
,
p^{-}_{z}=\sqrt{2}
,
h=0.025
,
N_{k}=64
, and
\omega=8+0.25\,\mathrm{i}
. As expected,
U_{\bm{h}}
depends on the choice of the augmented data and changes accordingly, whereas
u_{\bm{h}}
is visually invariant.

) for different values of

, oscillates more as

increases, and decays less at infinity as
\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits\omega
decreases.

7
Extensions and perspectives

In this section, we explore potential extensions and perspectives arising from the present work.

7.1
Direct extension to a source term

The approach developed in this paper could be extended to the classical Helmholtz equation

-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\nabla}\,\mathbb{A}\,\nabla u^{0}-\rho\,\omega^{2}\,u^{0}=f,\quad\textnormal{in}\ \ \mathbb{R}^{2},

where the source term
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
has a compact support in the
{\bm{e}}_{x}
–direction. In particular, using the fact that
\mathbb{A}({\bm{x}})=\mathbb{A}_{p}({\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{x}})
and
\rho({\bm{x}})=\rho_{p}({\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{x}})
, one may seek
u^{0}
under the form
u^{0}({\bm{x}})=U^{0}({\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{x}})
, where
U^{0}
satisfies

\displaystyle-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\bm{\nabla}}{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\bm{\nabla}\,U^{0}-\rho_{p}\,\omega^{2}\,U^{0}=F,\quad\textnormal{in}\ \ \mathbb{R}^{3},

with
F\in L^{2}_{\textit{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
being compactly supported in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{x}
–direction, and satisfying
F({\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\bm{x}})=f({\bm{x}})
for almost any
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Similarly to the procedure summarized in Section
1.2
and developed throughout the paper, the augmented equation satisfied by
U^{0}
may be solved as follows.

•

We first choose an augmented source term
F
such that
F(\cdot+\textnormal{{e}}_{2})=F
, so that
U^{0}(\cdot+\textnormal{{e}}_{2})=U^{0}
satisfies a strip problem with periodicity conditions with respect to
{z}_{2}
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l}\displaystyle-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut\bm{\nabla}}\,{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\bm{\nabla}\,U^{0}-\rho_{p}\,\omega^{2}\,U^{0}&F\quad\textnormal{in}\ \ \Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}},\\[12.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\displaystyle U^{0}\in H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}},{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}),\ \ \mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\bm{\nabla}\,U^{0}\in H_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}}(\mathbf{div};\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle\#$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle\#$}}}).\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

•

We apply a partial Floquet-Bloch transform with respect to
{z}_{1}
, to obtain a family of waveguide problems parameterized by
{k}\in[-\pi,\pi]
, and defined in the cylinder
\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}:=\mathbb{R}\times(0,1)^{2}
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l}\displaystyle-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})}\,{\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,\widehat{U}^{0}_{k}-\rho_{p}\,\omega^{2}\,\widehat{U}^{0}_{k}&\widehat{F}_{k}\quad\textnormal{in}\ \ \Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}},\\[12.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\displaystyle\widehat{U}^{0}_{k}\in H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}),\ \ \mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,\widehat{U}^{0}_{k}\in H_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\mathbf{div};\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}),\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

where
\widehat{F}_{k}:={\mathcal{F}}F(\cdot,{k})
is compactly supported in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{x}
–direction, because
F
is compactly supported in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{x}
–direction.

•

Finally, we use the DtN method developed in
[
23
,
11
,
13
,
14
]
to compute the waveguide solution
\widehat{U}^{0}_{k}
for any
{k}\in[-\pi,\pi]
. More precisely, assuming that
(a_{-},a_{+})\subset\mathbb{R}
is the support of
\widehat{F}_{k}
in the
\textnormal{{e}}_{x}
–direction, by analogy with Section
4.3
and the interface equation (
4.21
), we characterize the restriction of
\widehat{U}^{0}_{k}
to the interior domain
\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{\emph{int}}:=(a_{-},a_{+})\times(0,1)^{2}
as the solution of:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\ =\ }l}\displaystyle-\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})}\,{\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,\widehat{U}^{0}_{k}-\rho_{p}\,\omega^{2}\,\widehat{U}^{0}_{k}&\widehat{F}_{k}\quad\textnormal{in}\ \ \Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{\emph{int}},\\[12.0pt]
\pm\big({\mathbb{\Theta}}\,\mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,\widehat{U}^{0}_{k}\cdot\textnormal{{e}}_{x}\big)+\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.38193pt}\smash{\Lambda}}\vphantom{\Lambda}^{\pm}_{k}\,\widehat{U}^{0}_{k}&0,\quad\textnormal{on}\ \ \{{x}=a^{\pm}\},\\[12.0pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil\displaystyle\widehat{U}^{0}_{k}\in H^{1}_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{\emph{int}}),\ \ \mathbb{A}_{p}\!\mskip 1.0mu\prescript{{\mathrm{t}\,\mkern-3.0mu}}{}{\mathstrut{\mathbb{\Theta}}}\,(\bm{\nabla}+\mathrm{i}\mskip 1.5mu{k}\mskip 1.5mu\textnormal{{e}}_{1})\,\widehat{U}^{0}_{k}\in H_{{\mathbb{\Theta}}\mskip 1.5mu{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}}(\mathbf{div};\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}^{\emph{int}});\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}\right.

see Figure
29
. The conditions on
\{{x}=a^{\pm}\}
feature DtN operators
\widehat{\rule{0.0pt}{5.38193pt}\smash{\Lambda}}\vphantom{\Lambda}^{\pm}_{k}
which can be computed from solutions of half-guide problems set in the half-cylinders
\Omega_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.875}{$\displaystyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\textstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}{\scalebox{0.875}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\#}^{2}$}}}\cap\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\pm}
. Since the source term
\widehat{F}_{k}
vanishes in these half-cylinders, the half-guide problems can be solved using the local cell problems and the propagation operator introduced in Section

.
